              PROTECT COLLEGE SPORTS ACT OF 2026--Resumed

  The ACTING PRESIDENT pro tempore. Under the previous order, the 
Senate will resume consideration of S. 4668, which the clerk will 
report.
  The senior assistant bill clerk read as follows:

       A bill (S. 4668) to protect the name, image, and likeness 
     rights of, and provide protections for, student athletes and 
     to promote fair competition among intercollegiate athletics, 
     and for other purposes.


                   Recognition of the Majority Leader

  The ACTING PRESIDENT pro tempore. The majority leader is recognized.


                             Small Business

  Mr. THUNE. Madam President, so far this year, 4.3 million Americans 
have filed to start their own business. That is a 13-percent increase 
over the same time last year. As these entrepreneurs get underway, 
their businesses will be able to take advantage of the pro-growth tax 
reform the Republicans enacted last year in the Working Families Tax 
Cuts. Because we delivered permanent tax reform, an entire generation 
of American small business owners, entrepreneurs, and innovators will 
have a better environment in which to pursue their American dream.
  Small businesses are already benefiting from this landmark law. This 
year, 12 million small business owners saw a lower tax bill, a $7,000 
average tax cut. That is the result of lower tax rates across the 
board, the permanent 199A small business deduction, immediate expensing 
for new equipment and for factory construction, and the permanent 
research and development tax credit. These tax policies are helping 
small businesses across an array of industries as well as family farms 
and ranches across America.
  That is certainly true in my home State of South Dakota. An 
agricultural co-op in my State says the 199A small business deduction, 
which Republicans first implemented with the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act back 
in 2017, has allowed them to pass $140 million in tax savings back to 
South Dakota farmers over the last decade.
  A car dealership in Sioux Falls was able to put an addition on and 
hire 10 new employees thanks to the bonus depreciation provision.
  Just to the west, a rancher in Montana said, thanks to the Working 
Families Tax Cuts expensing provisions his ranch ``will be able to 
upgrade equipment, increasing [its] efficiency without being hit by a 
huge tax burden.''
  U.S. Metal Powders--a manufacturer in Palmerton, PA--has broken 
ground on a new production line that will allow the business to double 
the size of its workforce.
  A convenience store operator in Denver has said the Working Families 
Tax Cuts will help him double the size of one of his stores and reopen 
two others, allowing him to grow job opportunities at his company by 50 
percent.
  They are not the only ones. Businesses across the country have been 
able to make investments in new equipment, raise their employees' pay,

[[Page S5022]]

and create jobs because of the tax policy in Republicans' landmark 
bill. One thing that business owners invariably bring up when they talk 
about the Working Families Tax Cuts is what it does for their 
employees.
  They talk about how they have been able to increase pay--not to 
mention the fact that more of that pay stays in their employees' 
pockets.
  The Working Families Tax Cuts was responsible for no tax on tips and 
overtime, which allows hard-working people to keep more of their hard-
earned money.
  And then there are the benefits employers have been able to offer 
because of this legislation. A business owner in Chicago has made long-
term investments in paid maternity leave and a 401(k) matching program.
  Another company in Missouri is giving its employees a profit-sharing 
bonus. A number of employers are pledging to invest in the Trump 
Accounts that their employees have been able to set up for their 
children. And provisions in this legislation support employer efforts 
to offer childcare and help repay their employees' student loans.
  I said that the Working Families Tax Cuts provides tax relief that 
will help small businesses and family farms and ranches for years to 
come. And on that point, perhaps no policy is as significant as the 
death tax relief we provided in this bill.
  For a lot of family businesses, especially ag families, the death tax 
has cast a dark shadow over their dream. It is a cause for anxiety, 
especially among farmers and ranchers, businesses that might look well-
off on paper but are actually cash poor.
  Their wealth is tied up in land, tractors, combines, and livestock. 
But because that doesn't matter when it comes to the death tax, many 
families have had to sell off all or part of their operation just to 
pay the tax bill when a family member dies.
  Eliminating this fundamentally unfair tax has long been a priority of 
mine, and I am proud that the Working Families Tax Cuts permanently 
raised the exemption threshold on the death tax, which means that a lot 
more families will be protected from losing their livelihood when they 
lose a loved one.
  This was an important priority for Republicans last year, and it is 
encouraging to see farmers and ranchers breathing a sigh of relief with 
the burden of this tax off of their shoulders.
  One farmer in Iowa said:

       One of my goals when I started farming was to be able to 
     pass this farm on to the next generation, and I'm doing that. 
     I feel very good about that.

  A rancher in Montana said:

       Without the estate tax reforms . . . ranches like ours 
     would have likely had to decide whether to sell off cattle or 
     land just to cover the tax bill when it was time to pass it 
     on. Now we have a fair shot at keeping the whole ranch 
     together for the next generation.

  Thanks to this bill, family farms and ranches and family businesses 
have a fair shot at staying in the family for generations to come. 
These are just some of the results of the Working Families Tax Cuts, 
and I can't overstate how significant this bill is.
  The owners of a small business in Arizona wrote in their local paper 
last year that if Congress had not passed the Working Families Tax 
Cuts, they ``would have had to raise prices and cut costs, and over 
time, the pressure would have only increased.''
  They are not alone. This Republican majority has worked hard to put 
more money in American families' pockets, in many cases by getting 
government out of the way. We have delivered permanent--permanent--tax 
relief.
  We rescinded a number of Biden-era regulations that would have 
reduced choice and raised prices for Americans. We passed a housing 
bill that eliminated a lot of redtape that has driven up the cost of 
home ownership and implemented new measures to drive down prices at the 
pharmacy counter.
  Republicans are working to put more money in hard-working Americans' 
pockets and help America's small businesses grow. We are setting the 
conditions for a brighter future and keeping the American dream alive 
and strong for future generations.
  I yield the floor.
  I suggest the absence of a quorum.
  The ACTING PRESIDENT pro tempore. The clerk will call the roll.

                          Trump Administration

  Mr. SCHUMER. Madam President, this is on the taxpayer-funded Trump 
political ads.
  Every day--every day--Trump comes up with a new corruption scheme to 
abuse American taxpayers for his own benefit. That is why corruption is 
a hallmark of this administration. Yesterday, Trump aired another 
partisan campaign ad on multiple channels during prime time. And if you 
can believe it, who paid for it? The U.S. Government. The U.S. 
taxpayers paid for a political ad by Trump.
  These ads are virtually indistinguishable from those made for Trump's 
2024 campaign, and yet you the taxpayer is funding them.
  It is an absolute violation of what democracy is supposed to look 
like. Americans sat down to watch football, not to be force-fed Trump's 
propaganda on their own dime. Only Trump would have the audacity to 
force Americans not only to watch his propaganda, but to pay for it.
  We can add these campaign ads to the list of vanity projects that 
Trump has egregiously charged to the American taxpayer: his arch, his 
ballroom, upgrades to his luxury foreign jet, his MAGA slush fund, and 
so much more. The corruption goes on and on; it is endless in this 
administration. Every week, Trump thinks of a new corrupt measure so he 
can rip off the American taxpayer and enrich himself.
  I am calling on the administration to reveal which Agency aired this 
illegal propaganda, on whose orders, and exactly at what cost to the 
American taxpayer. These ads send a clear message, just not the one 
Trump intended.
  It sends the message that Trump and the Republicans are scared; they 
are desperate; and they know the American people are done buying what 
they are selling.


                              Rescissions

  Madam President, on the Trump rescissions, Trump has no problem 
wasting billions of American taxpayers on his vanity projects but 
suddenly gets stingy when there is an opportunity to support American 
families.
  Over the weekend, Trump illegally cut nearly a billion dollars from 
programs designed to help American families and communities to get 
ahead. Trump's rescissions package eviscerated funding that supports 
kids, K through 12 students, small businesses, health, and clean air 
research.
  These cuts are just another slap in the face to the American people 
as they struggle to afford the skyrocketing costs of Trump's never-
ending war and spiraling economy.
  Here is an idea, Donald: Instead of burning billions of taxpayer 
dollars on the arch or the reflecting pool or the ballroom or any of 
your hundred vanity projects, Trump, you ought to invest that money in 
American families and keep these programs intact.
  Trump cut funding that Congress approved--Democrats and Republicans. 
Every Senate Republican who voted for any of the bills providing this 
funding ought to be up in arms.
  Senate Democrats are going to challenge Trump's illegal cuts every 
chance we get. Unless Senate Republicans want to remain nothing more 
than a rubberstamp for Donald Trump and his grim reaper Russell Vought, 
they need to join us to stop the Trump administration from robbing the 
American people blind.


                                  Iran

  Madam President, finally on the Iran war, it now enters its eighth 
month, and Trump continues to drag America through the mess he has made 
in Iran. It was going to end in a week or a month or 2 weeks or 3 weeks 
or 2 months. We are in the eighth month.
  When Trump started his disastrous war, yes, he said it would only 
take a few weeks, but Trump's campaign is now entering its eighth 
month--its eighth month. Trump is starting the eighth month of his war 
the same way he started the first month, with empty promises to the 
American people that victory is right around the corner.
  Yesterday, Trump told FOX News:

       We're going to win this war very soon, and as soon as we 
     win it, the oil will go down.


[[Page S5023]]


  Well, it has been going up for 8 months, Mr. President. The American 
people are out billions of dollars out of their pockets already. Nobody 
wants to wait any longer.
  Whatever timeline Trump thinks is ``very soon,'' isn't soon enough 
for the American people. We are getting crushed by the cost of this war 
every time we go to the gas station. Trump shows no signs of ending 
this war, not when he just launched a pledge for new strikes on Iran 
after the midterms.
  How much longer is Trump going to continue his failed war? another 
month? another 7 months? another year?
  Trump's costly war with Iran will end whenever Trump Republicans find 
the courage to listen to the American people instead of the hysteria 
from the President.
  Republicans could have shown that courage last week by supporting our 
War Powers Resolution to stop the fighting in Iran and bring costs 
down. Instead, Senate Republicans chose to leave Americans waiting and 
voted for Trump's war to continue.
  Every American ought to ask these Senators whether appeasing the 
President is truly worth the cost of this horrible war.
  I yield the floor.
  The ACTING PRESIDENT pro tempore. The Democratic whip.


                          Trump Administration

  Mr. DURBIN. Madam President, before I start my remarks this 
afternoon, I would like to make note of two interesting events this 
weekend. I was asked to speak at a dinner in St. Louis right across the 
river from my hometown of East St. Louis, and it was known as a Harry 
Truman dinner.
  And I told a story about the former President from Independence, MO; 
that when he returned from Washington in 1953 to his home State of 
Missouri, he and his wife moved back into their home in Independence, 
and he reported to the press that when they arrived, he took the 
``grips'' up to the attic.
  For those who don't know the term ``grips,'' it was the word that was 
used to describe luggage. So former President Harry Truman was 
reporting that he was back home again now, and his life would be much 
the same.
  He proceeded to also tell how he had turned down many opportunities 
for payment, salaries, fees, rewards that were available to former 
Presidents even in the early 1950s.
  And he said at the time that as far as he was concerned, although it 
was perfectly legal to do the things he was asked to do--to serve on 
boards, for example, or be chairman of various undertakings, or even to 
play the piano on television, and he was offered money for that--he 
just didn't think it was appropriate.
  In his words, it reflected poorly on the prestige and integrity of 
the Office of the President, the prestige and integrity of the Office 
of the President.
  Doesn't that sound quaint these days, when we have a President who, 
in his first year of his second Presidential term, made $1.4 billion 
from his investments, particularly with the cryptocurrency bunch?
  Think about that for a second. Here are this President and his 
family, who are making billions of dollars--if you include the family--
from investments that directly relate to his office.
  The most recent one that was reported was just 2 weeks ago when 
Donald Trump, Jr., had his wedding reception on some island somewhere, 
and it leaked to the press that the entire festivity reception was paid 
for by a Russian oligarch who is close to Vladimir Putin.
  Surely, Senator Durbin, you are making this up.
  No, I am not. We are virtually at war with Putin and Russia for their 
invasion of Ukraine, and the son of the President of the United States 
gets married, goes to a private island for a lavish reception, and it 
is paid for by a Russian oligarch. That picture is so wrong from the 
start.
  As my colleague Senator  John Curtis, Republican of Utah, said when 
he heard of this: You expect a toaster for a wedding gift; you don't 
expect a Russian oligarch to pay for the reception. But that is what 
happened.
  I might add, Senator Curtis--and I praise him for this--said: Why 
wouldn't the Senate Judiciary Committee be looking into this?
  I have the same question. I approached the chairman of the committee 
and other members of the committee and said: You know, if such a thing 
had happened with Hunter Biden, for goodness' sake, you would have had 
much more than a hearing; you would have had a subpoena and depositions 
and God knows what.
  But the fact of the matter is, there is scarcely a peep when Senator 
Curtis, a Republican Member of the Senate, made what is a perfectly 
reasonable suggestion--that Congress look into any possibility of 
corruption and self-dealing when a member of the President's family 
receives such a lavish gift from a Russian oligarch.
  I might add so that the record is clear that Senator Curtis said he 
wanted Hunter Biden, son of President Joe Biden, to also face the same 
questions as to whether he used his connection with his father to make 
any money or to receive any gifts during the Biden Presidency. What was 
the reaction of Hunter Biden? He said: ``Bring it on.'' I have had two 
sworn depositions before Congress on the subject. I am happy and 
content with my answers, and I will do it again if necessary. And I 
will sit right next to Donald Trump, Jr. We can both testify under oath 
as to our personal experiences.
  That was Hunter Biden's response. What was Donald Trump, Jr.'s 
response? Crickets. Crickets. I wish I knew the Russian word for 
``crickets.'' Not a word. Nothing to suggest anything happened that was 
wrong. We know better. We know exactly what happened, what was wrong.
  I thought of the President one other time this week. I have a Ford F-
150 truck--it is an older truck--in the driveway of my home in 
Springfield, and I took it into the gas station to fill up. I am sure 
that most Americans had the same feeling I had at that moment: For 
God's sake, what is happening to gasoline prices? I believe I paid 
$4.59 a gallon for the lowest octane gas for my old truck. But I can 
just imagine what families and individuals are facing as they go to the 
gasoline pumps every day and witness these dramatic increases in 
gasoline and energy prices under the Trump administration.
  Why is this the case? Well, it is the case because we are in the 
eighth month of the first year of President Trump's war in Iran. Do you 
remember when he came to the American people and said he was going to 
invade Iran? Of course you don't. He didn't do that. He just did that. 
He ignored the Constitution, which gives Congress the power to declare 
war, and went forward and invaded and attacked Iran. We have lost--I 
believe 18 is the last count of brave American service men and women 
who have died as a result of that conflict. There is no end in sight.
  Every day you fill up your gas tank, a trucker fills up their diesel 
tank, a farmer goes out in the field--and we are harvesting now in 
Illinois--and sees the cost of diesel fuel, every day, you remember 
this is a creation of the President of the United States for a war that 
was unnecessary, illegal, and didn't follow the Constitution at its 
outset. No end in sight.
  The President has reported over 40 times that we have Iran on the 
ropes, that they can't wait to negotiate a settlement, and it never 
happens. This is affecting U.S. families and businesses, but it is 
affecting countries all over the world, this unnecessary and illegal 
war, as far as I am concerned.
  Now more and more, Republican Members of Congress, who have not stood 
up in Washington to assert the responsibility and right of Congress to 
declare war, are going back home, campaigning for reelection, and 
saying they got it wrong; war is a mistake. Where have they been for 8 
months while we have been having these votes on the floor as to whether 
Congress approves of the war of Iran? Democrats, but for one or two, 
consistently voted against this war. The Republicans now, in the 
closing days before the midterm election, have found religion on the 
subject.


                              Rescissions

  Madam President, on Friday night, the Trump administration announced 
the President has unilaterally rescinded nearly $1 billion in funds 
Congress had approved for spending on a bipartisan basis. This funding 
was meant for education programs, health research, assistance for 
businesses, immigration services, and support to other countries--
particularly those we have an obligation to help.

[[Page S5024]]

  This move is an illegal power grab by the Trump administration, plain 
and simple. We have seen this movie before.
  Last year, President Trump canceled $4.9 billion in foreign aid with 
his so-called pocket rescission maneuver. Earlier in 2025, my 
Republican colleagues supported President Trump's rescission request 
that clawed back $9 billion in foreign aid and public broadcast 
funding.
  Rescission requests are made under a fast-track process in which a 
President can request Congress to rescind Federal funding with a simple 
majority vote in each Chamber. The law requires that if Congress does 
not pass a bill rescinding the funding within 45 days, the President 
must release it.
  However, President Trump--surprise--is attempting an unlawful 
maneuver called the pocket rescission. He is seeking to rescind funding 
less than 45 days before the end of the fiscal year, believing this 
provides him with power to cancel funding with or without the approval 
of Congress.
  Here is the surprise ending to this scenario: He will be challenged 
in court, as he has been again and again and again. Overwhelmingly, he 
has lost in court with his ideas of what the Constitution really means 
again and again.
  The Government Accountability Office has stated that the President's 
pocket rescissions are illegal.
  Democrats fought for pocket rescission protection in the continuing 
resolution we passed earlier this year to prevent the White House from 
doing exactly what they have done. Not surprisingly, Republicans in the 
Senate and the House refused to stand for the law for fear that they 
would anger their President.
  My colleagues on the other side of the aisle might express some 
concern now about the items that have been cut by the administration, 
but where were they this summer when they had a chance to fix it?
  For Donald Trump, billion-dollar ballrooms are full speed ahead. 
Money for schools? Cut. Vanity projects like the Trump golden arch are 
priorities, but medical research is cut. Taxpayer-funded political ads 
of the wartime President looking very resolute are a must. Medical care 
for children? Cut.
  Over the past 20 months, Republicans have continuously bent the knee, 
kissed the ring, and handed Donald Trump more power and authority on a 
silver platter. Now the question is whether they will finally find 
their collective backbone and do something about this illegal Executive 
maneuver.


                       Special Counsel Jack Smith

  Madam President, tomorrow, the Senate Judiciary Committee is going to 
hear from Special Counsel Jack Smith. This is a historic event, and let 
me tell you why. Jack Smith is a nonpartisan career prosecutor 
appointed to investigate President Trump's efforts to overturn the 2020 
election as well as his effort to illegally retain highly classified 
government documents.
  In 2020, President Trump spent months spreading the Big Lie that the 
2020 election was stolen. I have heard this so many times, I can 
understand what was going on in the Soviet Union when the communists 
were in control. If they had an item in history that was embarrassing 
in the history of their country, they would simply change the history 
textbooks.
  Well, we have the same thing going on when it comes to the 2020 
election. This President, who cannot bring himself to even consider the 
possibility--the possibility--that he lost an election, continues to 
hammer away at the Big Lie, but he lost by 7 million votes. Joe Biden 
won the 2020 election.
  This President has pressured State officials, his own Vice President, 
the Justice Department, and Members of Congress to overturn the results 
of the election. This culminated in the January 6 attack on the 
Capitol, where President Trump's supporters tried to violently prevent 
the constitutional certification of the 2020 election.
  I was here, right here at my desk when this happened, when the mob 
started coming into this Chamber, and we filed out that door with 
police protection to avoid this effort to overturn the 2020 election. 
These MAGA Trump loyalists were trying to defy the Constitution, and 
they haven't quit to this day.
  Instead of condemning this egregious criminal conduct on January 6, 
my Republican colleagues are spreading baseless conspiracy theories and 
attacking career prosecutors and FBI agents, claiming the so-called 
deep state is out to get Donald Trump--the deep state.
  Now, after years of criticism and multiple hearings about the 
investigations, Senate Judiciary Republicans are finally holding a 
hearing with Jack Smith, the special counsel who investigated these 
matters.
  You say to yourself: Well, he must have resisted, hired some lawyers, 
fought the Republicans, refused to appear, wouldn't stand and answer 
questions under oath.
  Exactly the opposite is true. Jack Smith asked the Republicans in 
Congress, who continually berate him for his conduct, for an 
opportunity to testify under oath to the committee, the Judiciary 
Committee. He asked for that opportunity a year ago--October of 2025. 
They ignored it.
  At the time, Judiciary Committee Democrats--of which I am proud to 
say I am the ranking member--sent a letter urging the chairman of the 
Judiciary Committee to stop complaining about Jack Smith and bring him 
before the committee under oath. Stop making speeches and ask the man, 
who you believe to be responsible for wrongdoing, questions that he has 
to answer truthfully.
  He had agreed to do that a year ago. Yet they would rather give a 
speech than actually hear a man under oath defend his position.
  We made this request again in February of 2026. Well, soon, in just a 
few days, Jack Smith will appear under oath before the Judiciary 
Committee. The chairman had declined to bring him before, claiming they 
needed more time--more time--to gather information. Still, Republicans 
on the committee have made no effort to obtain a copy of volume 2 of 
Jack Smith's report, which details the findings from the criminal 
investigation into President Trump's alleged mishandling of classified 
materials.
  You remember the classified information that was found near the 
bathroom in Mar-a-Lago? I remember it.
  If this hearing is really about getting answers, we will finally get 
our chance--Jack Smith will get his chance to tell his side of the 
story under oath.
  Despite these impediments, I am glad my Republican colleagues will 
finally address these concerns directly. It is about time and, in fact, 
it is well past time for this committee, the Judiciary Committee, to 
move on and stop spreading political rumors and get down to the truth.
  I yield the floor.

  The Senator from Connecticut.


                                S. 4668

  Mr. MURPHY. Mr. President, the legislation that we're going to be 
voting on tonight is predicated on a simple idea, the idea that college 
sports is in crisis and that it requires a fairly extraordinary act of 
legislative intervention to address this crisis.
  I am speaking in opposition to this bill, and I think it is important 
to start by answering the question: Is college sports in crisis? Is it 
in chaos, as Senator Cruz is fond of saying?
  Well, the facts don't back up that characterization of college 
sports. I am a huge college sports fan. I watch a lot of college 
basketball, a little bit less but still a lot of college football, and 
I know that there are more people than ever showing up to these games 
and watching the games.
  The Big Ten just signed a $13 billion contract to broadcast its 
games. The revenue of the NCAA, broadly, is at an alltime high. The SEC 
and the Big Ten are both making over a billion dollars. Women's sports 
is growing--92,000 people recently filled the stadium to watch a 
women's volleyball match. Schools are adding, not subtracting, women's 
programs.
  In fact, more people are paying attention to college sports than ever 
before. College sports is growing both on the men's and the women's 
side.
  And so, what is this crisis? Well, the crisis, it appears to me, is 
pretty simple. Young athletes who perform a service that would be worth 
billions in the free market are finally getting what they lawfully 
deserve. A system

[[Page S5025]]

of exploitation--an illegal system of exploitation--in which the 
colleges and their industry allies colluded to keep as much of the $13 
billion that the college sports industry generates for themselves, that 
system started to crumble because courts--as high as the Supreme 
Court--came to the inevitable conclusion that that system of 
exploitation was illegal.
  Justice Kavanaugh, in a 9 to 0 decision, says the current NCAA model 
is ``suppressing the pay of student athletes who collectively generate 
billions of dollars in revenues for colleges [each] year.'' Ruling 9 to 
0, essentially, that that system was unlawful.
  And so this system of collusion by the colleges designed to keep the 
compensation of athletes as low as possible, it was ruled illegal is 
essentially what happened here.
  And the industry had two choices. They could either work out a new 
set of rules with the athletes. That is generally what has to happen if 
you want industry-wide rules that limit compensation, that limit that 
applicability of a free market. You have to negotiate with your 
employees--in this case the athletes--or you could come to Congress and 
get your illegal system deemed legal.
  And that is what we are essentially doing here today. We are taking a 
system that the Supreme Court said could not persist under our law, and 
we are changing the law to allow that illegal system to persist.
  How do we know that this bill is about solving that narrow crisis 
that I identified, that crisis of college athletes finally making 
money, and the adults in charge of the system not liking that the kids 
were making money?
  Well, the first way we know that is that the bill does really nothing 
to stop the runaway overall costs of college athletics, in particular, 
some of the biggest drivers of costs being facilities and the salaries 
of adults. Let us just take one example. And I see my friend from 
Louisiana is on the floor, currently.
  Just last week, LSU's president said the school's sports program is 
projected to carry a $26 million deficit, and the president said ``We 
have got to stop that bleeding.'' A $26 million deficit.
  But LSU just agreed to pay their new coach Lane Kiffin $91 million 
over 7 years, along with other bonuses to him like 65 hours of private 
jet travel, temporary housing, courtesy vehicles. LSU also just paid a 
buyout of over $53 million to the coach they fired. In fact, last year, 
the buyouts for just three coaches at LSU totaled over $169 million, 
six times the annual operating deficit of the entire athletics program.
  So if you want to know what is driving the deficit at these big 
schools, it is not simply the amount of money they are paying the 
students. Those numbers are dwarfed by the amount of money that is 
going to coaches and the facilities.
  But yet, this bill constricts only--only caps--the money going to the 
players. It does nothing to cap the money going to the coaches. It does 
nothing to stop the continued billions of dollars going in to feed this 
facilities' arms race.
  Second, what about the antitrust exemption? Why is that in the bill? 
Well, certainly not to help athletes. This antitrust exemption will 
allow schools in some cases to continue their practices that compromise 
athlete safety because now they have a broad antitrust exemption to do 
that. Perhaps we will pass an amendment that will help with that later 
today.
  What it primarily is in the bill for is to allow the schools to be 
able to bargain collectively in order to get a better TV deal. If the 
schools don't have to worry about antitrust law, one of the things they 
can do is join together across conferences to bargain for additional TV 
revenue. And in this bill, only one-fifth of the revenue that comes in 
will have to be shared with the athletes. Whereas, in the professional 
leagues, about 50 percent of the revenue, including TV revenue, goes to 
the athletes.
  So, the antitrust exemption allows the schools to make a whole lot 
more money, and other provisions in the bill allow the schools to keep 
that money for themselves rather than share it in the same way that 
professional leagues might share it with the athletes.
  And then what about the transfer restrictions in the bill? Why do 
those only apply to players? I admit that the current system in which 
players can move from one school to the next every year is probably not 
in the best long-term interests of the sport.
  But if the goal is really to control costs, that provision would 
apply to coaches as well because one of the ways costs go up is that 
coaches can freely move after every season from one program to the 
other, bidding schools against each other.
  This bill says that a coach can't leave in the middle of the season, 
but that almost never happens.
  The reality is this bill only says players can only transfer once 
without penalty. It doesn't say anything about coaches.
  Now, as I said, I think that there are rules inside college athletics 
that don't make a lot of sense right now: unlimited transfers; the 
potential ability for pro players to come back to college. These agent 
fees look ridiculous to me.
  But you don't need a bill of this sweeping scope to address what are 
a set of minor but important practices in college athletics that have 
gotten out of control.
  We could have either passed a very narrow bill here to address the 
aging issue and the transfer issue, or we could have done what happens 
in most industries which is just say to the NCAA and to the colleges: 
Tough luck. Congress isn't going to come in here and micromanage your 
relationship with the athletes. If you want to work something out, sit 
down and talk to the athletes about it. If you want rules that apply 
across the industry, then do what every other industry does, which is 
have a dialogue with the workers.
  Now, whether you want to admit that the athletes are workers or not, 
the Supreme Court has basically ruled that they need to be treated as 
such for compensation purposes.
  And so we have two other options besides this sweeping bill that, in 
my belief, preserves this system of exploitation. We could have either 
passed a much more narrow bill that I think would have gotten even 
broader consensus here, would have had an easier time passing the 
House, or we could have just said: You know what? Congress doesn't want 
to get involved in litigating the details of the arrangement between 
players and coaches and schools. You guys work that out, just like we 
require every other industry to do this.
  I think in the end, this bill, if it passes and becomes law, is 
actually going to be a long-term threat to college athletics. And I 
will just end here.
  In the short run, I get it. It makes coaches' lives easier. They 
don't have to worry about players leaving if they are treated badly, if 
they are not compensated adequately. By Congress stepping in, dictating 
the terms with which students can play college athletics, it empowers 
coaches. But especially in football, where every single additional year 
you play, you are doing more damage to your brain, more damage to your 
ability to make money once you get into the pros, students are not 
dumb. They are not going to sit in college sports with a cap on their 
earnings and currently an inability to get to the pros until they have 
played 3 years of amateur athletics. They will find a way around 
college sports. That will be the primary impact of this artificial cap 
on compensation, this massive antitrust exemption for colleges. I think 
it may well, in the end, be the unwinding of college sports.

  The way to save college sports is to admit that these kids provide an 
incredibly valuable service, as the Supreme Court has judged as well, 
and they deserve to be fairly compensated. That, in the long run, would 
have been the best way to have made sure that we preserve college 
sports, which I agree with my colleagues is worth preserving. One of 
the most basic, fundamental American pastimes is the ability for us to 
come together, no matter our religion, our race, or our political 
affiliation, to root for our State's team. That binds us together as 
Americans.
  In the end, I think this bill--maybe not in the first few years but 
in the long run--will be a greater threat to the stability of college 
sports than doing nothing.
  I yield the floor.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Louisiana.

[[Page S5026]]

  

  Mr. KENNEDY. Mr. President, I want to say a few words, first, about 
the Protect College Sports Act that Chris just talked about.
  I am not going to go into a lot of detail about the rules. Basically, 
it will set about--the bill, rather. Basically, it will establish one 
nationwide set of rules mainly pertaining to NIL payments--name, image, 
and likeness payments--for college athletes; the ability of an athlete 
to transfer between schools and the ability of an athlete to receive 
some of the money that athletics generate at that particular athlete's 
university. But if you look at this bill from 35,000 feet, what it 
really does is exempt colleges and universities and the NCAA from the 
antitrust laws. We are all subject to the antitrust laws. This bill 
would basically say that colleges and universities and the NCAA aren't.
  What does that mean? Well, let me give you an example.
  Let's suppose that all of the plumbing companies in my State that 
employ plumbers got together--that the owners of the plumbing companies 
got together, that they met this weekend--and said: We all employ 
plumbers, and the more we pay the plumbers whom we employ, the less 
profit we as the owners of the plumbing companies make. So let's all 
agree as owners of the plumbing companies to not pay our plumbers more 
than 25 bucks an hour.
  Well, that is illegal. It is price-fixing. It violates the antitrust 
laws. You know, if the owners of the plumbing companies did that, at a 
minimum, they would have to pay big fines, and they might get sent to 
prison and spend some quality time with Bubba. So that is illegal.
  The same principle would apply if colleges and universities and their 
athletic programs got together and agreed on what athletes could make 
on NIL and salaries and how many times they could transfer. This bill 
exempts the colleges and universities and the athletic programs from 
the antitrust laws. I mean, that is what it does, and it sets rules 
about what the colleges and the athletic programs have to pay in terms 
of NIL payments and salaries and the number of times they can transfer.
  Now, I love college sports--I watch them all the time--and we all 
want what is best for our teams. You may like what the bill does or you 
may dislike it, but what it does is give the NCAA and college sports 
teams and the universities an exemption from the antitrust laws.
  Now, who wins from this bill?
  Well, obviously, the universities and the colleges, OK? This is going 
to save them money.
  I will tell you who else wins, not that they need Congress' help: our 
college coaches. We have got four college coaches--football coaches--in 
America who are making $13 million a year, not over the life of their 
contracts--a year. They are good coaches, but they are pretty fat and 
happy--not fat physically, but they are happy. ``Satisfied'' would be a 
better word. This makes it easier for them to recruit. This also helps 
the colleges and the coaches in that they don't have to raise as much 
money from donors to pay athletes.
  Now, the proponents of this bill also say that it helps the athletes. 
I can see where it does in some respects, but I can see where it caps 
their earnings in other respects, OK? Maybe, in the long run, if it 
makes college sports healthy, it will be in the best interests of the 
athletes so I am not going to weigh in on that. You can read the bill 
or read the articles and decide for yourself whether it really helps 
the athletes or not. My friend Senator Murphy just gave a very eloquent 
talk about how it doesn't. But the point I am trying to make is, if we 
give the NCAA and we give the colleges and universities an exemption 
from the antitrust laws, it is going to cap the amount that these 
athletes can make.
  Let me assume, for a second that, in the long run, it is a positive 
for the athletes. So the athletes win. The coaches win. The colleges 
and the universities win. What is wrong with this picture? Who makes 
all this possible? The fans. They are the people who pay to go to the 
games--the fans--the ordinary Americans out there who have to make 
donations to the athletic programs in order to even have the privilege 
of buying the tickets. At some of our colleges and universities--I am 
thinking of college football--it is so popular that, in order to be 
eligible for the privilege of buying the tickets, you have to make as 
much as a $10,000 or $20,000 contribution to the university first.

  So the fans are paying their fair share, but as best as I can tell, 
this bill doesn't do a damned thing for the fans. Colleges and 
universities, particularly since they will now have more money after 
being able to cap the amount that the athletes make, are saving money. 
You would think they would want to go cut the ticket prices to keep 
their fans happy, but the ticket prices go up, up, up. We are not doing 
anything for the fans.
  Now, I am not saying this is a bad bill. It is not a perfect bill, 
but I am not saying it is a bad bill. I am saying it could be made 
better. Republicans want to offer four amendments to try to make the 
bill better. My Democratic friends want to offer four amendments. I am 
not blaming anybody, but I think both sides--Democrats and 
Republicans--are going to be blocked from making amendments, from 
offering amendments, and that is not how this place is supposed to 
work. I am not blaming anybody. There has been a lot of negotiations 
going on, but it is a dadgum shame. We ought to let Senators be 
Senators. We ought to let Republicans offer amendments and Democrats 
offer amendments and Independents offer amendments to try to make this 
bill better. We shouldn't have to swallow this thing without offering 
our input, but I think that is how it is going to end up, and I am not 
happy about it.
  One of those amendments--you will not be surprised to learn--is mine, 
and if you give me the chance, I am going to offer an amendment to do 
something for the people who make all of this money possible for the 
universities. And the people who make it possible for college football 
coaches--I am not begrudging them--to make $13 million a year--did I 
mention some make $13 million a year? That is not over the life of 
their contracts. That is a year--are the fans. If I am given the 
chance--and it doesn't look like I am going to be able to--I am going 
to offer an amendment that says, in return for the antitrust exemption 
and capping the amount of money that colleges and universities and 
coaches have to pay their athletes, you can't raise ticket prices on 
the fans more than 4 percent a year.
  Let's do something for the fans, but I don't know if I am going to 
get that opportunity. I may offer it in a separate bill, but they 
probably won't let me bring it up, and I am going to just mention that.


                             Affordability

  Mr. President, No. 2, we had the chance this week to lower prices for 
cargo ships, oil tankers, fishing vessels, lawnmowers, for people who 
own lawnmowers, for people who own chain saws, and so much more. And it 
is legal malpractice if we don't do it.
  What am I talking about?
  When the bureaucracy promulgates a rule--and the bureaucracy under 
President Biden promulgated several rules which raised prices on cargo 
ships, oil tankers, fishing vessels, lawnmowers, chain saws, and other 
things--their rules make the cost of these things go up.
  Now, do you know who paid that increased cost? It wasn't the cargo 
ship owners, et cetera, et cetera. You and I did. The people did--the 
people who buy the lawnmowers.
  I don't know who the people were in the Biden administration who came 
up with this idea, but this is the kind of stuff that causes inflation. 
I don't know who came up with it, so this isn't personal, but I will 
say whatever rock star in the Biden administration came up with these 
rules is like a rock, only dumber. Whatever rock star in the Biden 
administration came up with these rules to raise prices on the American 
people is living proof as to why it took our ancestors tens of 
thousands of years to learn how to stand upright. Whatever rock stars 
in the Biden administration came up with these rules to increase our 
prices are living proof that evolution is a slow, slow process.
  We have got the chance this week--through what we call CRAs--to get 
rid of these rules. If we don't act this week, the rules stay in place, 
and the American people pay more for lawnmowers and chain saws and 
fishing vessels and oil tankers and cargo ships,

[[Page S5027]]

and I could go on. All we have to do is take a vote this week. There is 
a time limit--this week.
  Now, I don't control the floor of the U.S. Senate. If I did, we would 
be voting to get rid of these rules like a hobo on a ham sandwich, but 
I don't control the Senate. I am not putting anybody down, but all I am 
saying is, it is legal malpractice, legislative malpractice, if the 
U.S. Senate is not allowed this week to vote to kill these rules. All 
of us have been out there, talking about the cost of living and how we 
need to get it down. Well, here is a chance. All we have got to do is 
vote. We ought to stay here until we vote.
  No. 3, I just want to make this clear. We are going to look back 5 
years from now and realize that what President Trump has done in Iran 
and what Israel has done in Iran has made the world immeasurably safer.
  And I know all about it. I know that it has caused gas prices to go 
up. I get it, and I am not happy about any of that. But what the 
President has done and Israel has done--and they had the courage to do 
it--has made the world safer--not just the Middle East, the entire 
world.
  Israel and America have gutted Iran like a fish. We didn't want to. 
It is not being reported by the press, but Iran today is immeasurably 
weaker. Iran today--both the public and the private sector--is a 
sucking chest wound. There are whole parts of Iran that look like 
something out of ``Mad Max'' 4.
  We have destroyed their air force; they have no air force. We have 
destroyed their navy; they have no navy, except they have got these 
little weenie boats left that they run around in in the strait. They 
have no navy.
  We have destroyed a lot of their missiles. We haven't been able to 
get all of them because they are hiding some of them in a cave. We have 
destroyed their weapons-producing facilities.
  I hate to see us have to do it to the Iranian people, but they have 
got 70 percent inflation. We are blockading everything going in and 
out. They can't sell their oil. Food is not getting in. Nothing is 
getting in because we are blockading it.
  We have increased sanctions. We just increased sanctions even more so 
that planes can't fly in and out of Iran.
  We have destroyed their nuclear weapons program. They have still got 
fissile material to make a nuclear warhead, but it is buried beneath 
the earth.
  Iran has no air defenses anymore. What does that mean? That means 
that we can send our bombers and fly anywhere we want to in our planes 
in Iran without worrying about being shot at--anywhere.
  Right now, Iran doesn't have a nuclear weapons program. What the 
President wants is for them to promise not to do it in the future. But 
right now, they don't have one. Do you know why? Because we ate them up 
and spit the bones on the floor. We had to do that. Our intelligence 
and Israeli intelligence will know within 5 minutes if Iran restarts 
its nuclear weapons program, and as soon as they do, because we have 
crushed them like a bug, we will know--and they have no air defenses--
and we can go in and bomb their program all over again.
  I wish we didn't have to do all of this, but you can't let these 
whack jobs have a nuclear weapon. These people are like--I am sorry. I 
am not talking about the good people of Iran; I am talking about the 
leadership. They are like Son of Sam. They are like Charles Manson. 
They think they are on a mission from God, that God has told them to 
kill everybody who disagrees with their radical form of Islam--kill 
them all, including Americans, and drink our blood out of a boot.
  They can't have nuclear weapons, and thanks to President Trump, they 
don't and they won't.
  (Ms. LUMMIS assumed the Chair.)
  Now, I know the price of gas. I get it. It is painful as hell. And I 
know it has increased inflation, and we are working to get it down.
  I know a lot of my colleagues--they have the right to their opinion--
have accused the President of everything except abandoning his children 
to wolves in terms of Iran. President Trump has made the world 
immeasurably safer--not just the Middle East--immeasurably safer 
because he is the first President in our lifetime, along with Israel, 
to say: Iran, we have had enough of your BS.
  We have gutted them like a fish, and I am proud of what American 
did--didn't want to do it, but I am very, very proud of it.
  Madam President, my work here is done. I can show myself out. If you 
like what you have heard today, I hope you will tell--what I have said 
today, if you like what I have said today, I hope you will tell your 
friends. If you don't like what I said today, I hope you will keep it 
to yourself.
  With that, I yield to my good friend Senator Coons.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Delaware.


                                S. 4668

  Mr. COONS. Madam President, I rise today to speak about the Protect 
College Sports Act as one of the 10 cosponsors of this bill. A question 
I asked myself when I first got involved in this issue, now some time 
ago, was, since I didn't play college sports myself: What would I want 
if my child or the child of a good friend of mine were playing college 
sports? What would I hope for for them in terms of protections--
protection from people pretending to be agents showing up at the home 
of a family with a really capable, really promising young athlete but 
who have no experience in negotiations? What would I want in terms of 
protection from agents? What about their ability to continue their 
college education if they get recruited to a promising school as a 
promising athlete but three games into the season, they tear their ACL? 
What about the opportunity to play an Olympic sport or play DII or 
DIII? What about future care for a lifelong injury?
  All of those and more are protected in this bill.
  One of the things that brought me to supporting this bill was the 
rough-hewn bipartisan compromise, which is the essence of what 
legislating here is, that provides a 10-year guarantee to finish out 
the scholarship that drew a young athlete to a school regardless of 
changing a major or leaving for a few years or having an injury; 
wraparound healthcare for the 5 years they are playing and for 5 years 
after playing for any injury related to college sports; and then 
lifetime medical trusts; and then last, registration, certification, 
and transparency for agents and a cap on the rate they can charge.
  This bill makes all of those protections real. In some ways, most 
importantly, it enshrined in statute revenue sharing and NIL 
compensation--name, image, and likeness compensation--for college 
athletes. It more than doubles money currently available for athletic 
programs to share with student-athletes, and it has a $5 million fund 
for women's and Olympic sports.
  As I have said, there are a number of protections for college 
athletes on the field. One that hasn't been mentioned much here is 
return-to-play decisions that, when made, have to be made by a medical 
professional whose responsibility is to the athlete--not by the coach, 
not by the athletic director.
  All the things I just cited--the scholarship protection, the 
healthcare protection, the return-to-play decision, the transparency 
and certification of agents--there are new Federal private rights of 
action, the ability to sue. If these aren't provided, if colleges make 
these promises and don't keep them, you can sue as a college athlete.
  This bill does not solve the question of collective bargaining. I 
support collective bargaining. I think we should have collective 
bargaining to protect the rights of college athletes. This bill is 
silent on that question.
  It also explicitly protects a majority of current lawsuits that have 
been filed and that will continue to go forward.
  In a few moments, it is my hope that we will lock in a whole series 
of amendment votes, and one of the amendments would make perfectly 
clear the protections that are already in this bill for allowing future 
lawsuits--lawsuits based on State tort claims that don't conflict with 
this bill, for things like hazing or abuse or CTE injury.
  If you just happen to be the best wrestler or the best fencer or the 
best rower in a small town in a rural part of America and you are 
looking to get a scholarship for college and an agent pitches up on 
your doorstep and makes a story to you, this bill protects you.
  In my view, this is an example of what bipartisan compromise can

[[Page S5028]]

produce. I just wanted to say I am grateful to Senators Cruz and 
Cantwell for their extensive discussions with stakeholders. This is not 
a dream Republican or Democratic bill, but it will protect the dreams 
of more than half a million young American athletes across our country. 
That is why it is supported by the U.S. Olympic Committee, by 
Olympians, by those who became Olympians playing college sports, the 
Team USA Athletes' Commission. It is supported by the players unions 
that are professional--the National Basketball Players Association, the 
NFL Players Association--and by the DI, DII, and DIII NCAA Student-
Athlete Advisory Committee chairs.
  It is my hope that we will soon vote on an amendment offered by my 
colleagues Senators Durbin and Baldwin that will clarify the intention 
of this bill, that will make it clear that State law claims don't 
conflict with this bill and survive and that student-athletes can sue 
schools, conferences, and the NCAA for misconduct.
  At the end of the day, when we gather as families later this fall at 
Thanksgiving, I don't know about your family, but mine will avoid 
painful and difficult conversations about political differences, 
religious differences, or even who likes which stuffing, but we will 
gather to watch college sports. At the end of the day, the chaos that 
has been caused by transfers, by eligibility, by uncertainty about the 
future, and by the destruction of conferences will be lessened by this 
bill.
  There is, for all of us who are fans of college athletics, a moment 
and an urgency to act, and this bill, this bipartisan bill, deserves 
passage in this body today.
  I yield the floor.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Missouri.
  Mr. SCHMITT. Madam President, I rise today to speak about the future 
of college sports and the legislation before us to protect it.
  The Protect College Sports Act is built around a simple idea: 
athletes should be able to benefit from their talent, they should be 
protected from people who want to take advantage of them, and every 
school and every athlete should play by the same set of rules.
  NIL revenue sharing and the transfer portal are here to stay. We are 
not turning back the clock; and whether you loved or hated the old 
system, we shouldn't try.
  The problem is that college sports entered this new era without a 
stable framework to govern it. One court issues an order, one State 
passes a law, one conference adopts a rule, another court blocks it. 
Before athletes, coaches, and schools can adjust, the rules change all 
over again. This is the contradiction facing college athletics today.
  The product is booming, but the model is breaking. Stadiums are 
packed, television audiences are strong, and billions of dollars are 
moving through the system; but behind the scenes, athletes and schools 
are trying to operate under a patchwork of State laws, court rulings, 
and conflicting standards. The Protect College Sports Act replaces that 
uncertainty with one national framework.
  An athlete in Missouri should have the same basic rights and 
protections as an athlete in California, Florida, or anywhere else in 
this country. A school should not gain an advantage simply because its 
State legislature passed a different NIL law. And a national 
competition should not be governed by 50 different rule books--that 
does not work in practice.
  This bill establishes clear national rules for NIL transfers, 
eligibility, recruiting, and revenue sharing. It gives the governing 
body targeted authority to enforce those rules because rules only 
matter if someone can actually enforce them.
  Just as important, athletes can keep making money. Let's be clear: 
this bill does not end NIL. It protects an athlete's right to earn 
money from his or her name, image, and likeness in Federal law for the 
first time. Athletes can still sign legitimate endorsement deals, work 
with local businesses and national brands, or hire an agent or an 
attorney. And schools can continue sharing substantial revenue directly 
with their student-athletes.
  What the bill does is put protections around that marketplace--a 
marketplace that already exists. Recently Kedrick Green, a football 
player at Howard, shared his story about having the right guardrails 
around earning NIL compensation and how stability helps an athlete 
compete and grow.
  Our bill fixes that by requiring agents to register, caps endorsement 
commissions, requires written contracts, and gives athletes redress for 
grievances against bad actors. There are instances right now where some 
of these shady agents will charge 30 or 40 percent of a deal--maybe 
even earnings for the lifetime of the student-athlete, even if they 
never go pro. That is not taking power away from athletes. That is 
giving them protections they simply do not have right now.
  The bill also restores basic structure to transfers and eligibility. 
Every athlete is guaranteed one transfer without losing eligibility.
  And the bill also recognizes that life happens. If a head coach 
leaves or if a sport is eliminated, that athlete can transfer again 
without sitting out. But unlimited free agency every semester is not 
sustainable. An athlete can suit up for your favorite team in the fall 
and be playing for a rival in the spring.
  And don't get me wrong, players deserve the freedom. If you are a 
backup somewhere, you should have a chance to go somewhere else. But 
they deserve stability also to build relationships, earn a degree, 
develop as athletes, and become part of a team.
  Lance Holtzclaw, a football player at Utah said it best. He said:

       I believe strongly in preserving these opportunities for 
     future generations while continuing to improve a system that 
     serves the student-athletes at its center.

  Our bill strikes that balance.
  It also establishes a clear 5-year eligibility clock with reasonable 
exceptions. College sports should be played by college athletes. Madam 
President, 27-year olds who go pro and come back to compete against 17-
year-olds is a joke, and that is completely acceptable right now under 
the current nonframework that we have. This should not be 
controversial.
  The bill also draws a clear line between legitimate NIL and buying a 
roster under the table. Real endorsement deals remain protected, but 
schools, boosters, and associated entities cannot disguise recruiting 
payments as marketing deals or use side arrangements to blow through 
the revenue-sharing cap. That is one of the chief criticisms right now 
under the House settlement that this bill actually builds upon from 
$21.5 million to almost $50 million a year per school every year, is at 
that $21.5 million number. That is not real. There are football rosters 
out there that are worth $40 million or $50 million, so let's get this 
thing under one legitimate framework.
  The bill creates a retention fund so schools can reward athletes who 
have already invested a full season in their program. It cannot be used 
to buy high school recruits. It cannot be used to lure first-time 
transfers. It rewards the athlete who played, stayed, developed, and 
became a leader in that locker room.
  Take Jaden Rashada promised $14 million by Florida. The deal 
eventually was canceled, the money was never delivered, and the Gator 
Collective behind it disappeared. The student-athlete was made a 
promise, then was left high and dry. That athlete's experience is the 
human cost of a system without any guardrails.
  Real empowerment means more than telling a teenager he is free to 
sign a contract. It means the contract is in writing, the agent is 
registered, the athlete knows what the agent can charge, and there is 
somewhere to turn when someone lies.
  Tradition is interwoven into the DNA of college sports. It is a 
uniquely American thing. This thing that we get to enjoy right now on a 
Saturday afternoon when the Florida Gators come to Missouri this 
Saturday--and I will be there--is unlike anything that exists anywhere.
  In Columbia, Mizzou football games, you can hear ``Mr. Brightside'' 
playing, bringing all 70,000 Tiger fans to their feet and singing the 
infamous chorus in a nod to those over in Kansas.
  In Iowa City after the first quarter at Kinnick Stadium, Hawkeye fans 
turn and wave to the children who are battling illness on the 12th 
floor at the Iowa Children's Hospital as a powerful symbol of 
community.

[[Page S5029]]

  Pittsburg State in Kansas has the gorilla walk through thousands of 
fans that are lining the path of the stadium to cheer on the football 
team, which is a big Division II power--at least was--entering to the 
song ``Welcome to the Jungle.''
  College sports are not only special because 100,000 people fill these 
SEC stadiums. They are special because hundreds of campuses have 
traditions that could not be replicated anywhere else. That is what 
makes college sports different. People do not fill stadiums simply to 
watch uniforms. Those uniforms represent a school, a State, a family 
tradition, and a community.
  And the opportunities created by college sports extend far beyond the 
stars that we see on Saturday afternoon or during March Madness or 
during the college baseball World Series. They reach swimmers, 
wrestlers, gymnasts, track athletes, softball, and volleyball players, 
first-generation students, and future Olympians. If the current arms 
race continues without limits and without new revenue, those 
opportunities will be the first to go.
  Clemson track and field athlete Ella Brooks said it best: It has 
afforded her opportunities she never would have imagined--including the 
ability to come to DC and help advocate for this bill that directly 
affects her and her younger brother and sister, who dreamed of playing 
at the collegiate level.
  College sports needs to be able to sustain itself or else future 
athletes like her younger siblings will not be afforded the same 
opportunities that she has received. That is what this bill does. That 
is the purpose of all of this, is to establish rules.
  It also gives schools an opportunity to generate more revenue. This 
is a provision that actually isn't talked about a lot, but if we are 
interested in growing the pie to save women's sports and Olympic 
sports, there ought to be an opportunity to maximize the value when the 
conferences go to rights holders and negotiate TV deals.
  Today, college media rights are fragmented. Conferences negotiate 
separately at different times and under different agreements.
  Meanwhile, schools are being asked to do more to preserve the Olympic 
and nonrevenue sports that create opportunities for thousands of young 
men and women--500,000 student-athletes in this country. Our first 
answer cannot always be higher ticket prices, another donor check, or 
cutting another sport.
  The Sports Broadcasting Act in title II of this gives schools another 
option. It allows conferences and schools to voluntarily--only if they 
choose to do so--to voluntarily pool their media rights and negotiate a 
larger package. Nobody is forced to participate in this. We will see. 
If we get this thing done, we will see how this plays out, but at least 
the option exists.
  Congress does not decide what the games are worth, the NCAA does not 
take control of anyone's media rights, and existing contracts are 
protected in this bill. We are simply giving schools another tool to 
capture the full value of the product millions of Americans already 
love. Instead of always finding new ways to spend more, let's give 
schools a way to grow the pie.
  Before I close, I want to address a few claims about the bill. Some 
say it gives the NCAA a blank check. It does not. Congress sets the 
guardrails and provides targeted legal protection only in the areas 
where national rules are necessary. The NCAA does not receive unlimited 
immunity.
  Some say the bill takes NIL rights away from athletes. The opposite 
is true. It protects the right to earn NIL compensation in Federal law, 
while stopping pay-for-play disguised as NIL and recruiting inducements 
dressed up as marketing.
  Some say the transfer provisions trap athletes at a school. They do 
not. Every athlete receives the ability to transfer without losing 
eligibility, plus additional protections for coaching change, graduate 
study, a discontinued sport, or athlete safety. That is a reasonable 
balance between player freedom and the stability every team sport 
needs.
  Those who want the broken system to continue claim this hinders 
college sports growth. The bill preserves title IX and protects 
scholarship and roster opportunities in nonrevenue sports. Doing 
nothing is not protecting these student-athletes.
  I would also point out that for the first time in Federal law, 
healthcare is guaranteed to athletes not only when they are in school, 
but when they are out of school. Players who are injured cannot lose 
their scholarship because of an injury.
  These are things that if you would have talked about back when I was 
playing or even 10 years ago, would have been sort of unthinkable, and 
we are protecting them.
  And some say Congress should simply stay out of college sports. Well, 
I understand that instinct. You would not have seen me giving this 
speech on the Senate floor 3 years ago when I got here. I would have 
been very, very skeptical of that.
  But the way this has played out, with, like I said, 27-year-olds 
competing with 17-year-olds, unlimited transfers, the agents--who are, 
by the way, the most vocal opponents of this bill, the ones wearing 
Gucci suits and alligator shoes. Not all of them, but some of them take 
advantage of these student-athletes. They don't like it because right 
now, they get 30 to 40 percent on a contract.
  By the way, they just tell the kid: Transfer every year. They may end 
up in a worse situation. They most certainly probably don't end up with 
a college degree. But, hey, you churned it; you got your cut.
  But the way it has played out, the only entity literally on planet 
Earth that can grant the limited antitrust protection status for the 
NCAA to enforce rules is Congress. It is time for this place to meet a 
moment.
  One of the biggest criticisms that we have here is that we don't 
really do anything that addresses the concerns that people have. And if 
you don't think people talk about this back home, I don't know who you 
are talking to because the people I talk to, they care. They might have 
some different data point that they see, but they know this thing is 
screwed up, and they would like to know that Republicans and Democrats 
could actually agree on something here to fix a problem that is so 
obvious. Or do we play the safe political games.
  Look, we can do the ``Thunderdome'' thing. We do it all the time on 
other stuff. But this is a chance, with a huge bipartisan margin in the 
U.S. Senate, to send a bill out of this Chamber, over to the House--
that the President supports--that can become law. That would be a 
significant accomplishment for a uniquely American institution, as I 
said--college sports.
  Congress should not call plays--as much as some of my colleagues 
might like to call an offensive coordinator from time to time--or run 
an athletic department, but only Congress can establish that national 
framework that applies across State lines and survives the constant 
cycle of litigation.
  You will hear complaints about this legislation, how it doesn't do 
this or that. Well, welcome to Congress. It is not a perfect bill. I 
have filed perfect bills. I have a lot of perfect bills that I have 
filed. Rarely do you vote on a perfect bill because what you are trying 
to do is discover the art of the possible, which is what we are doing 
here and what is before this Chamber now.
  They say that if you have opposition from both sides, you probably 
have a good bill. I don't know. We have really worked hard to address 
the concerns of many--almost everyone--for the issues they have 
identified here. That is part of legislating. And what we have here is 
a bill to protect and save a cherished American institution.
  If Congress does nothing, if we abdicate our responsibility on this 
issue, as we have on so many, the college athletes will not be the ones 
who come out on top. It will be the lawyers, the agents, consultants, 
the middlemen profiting from disorder. And all of us fans will pay the 
price too.
  The status quo no longer works. The Protect College Sports Act 
creates one national standard. It protects an athlete's right to earn. 
It protects athletes from exploitation. It restores clear rules. And 
through the Sports Broadcasting Act, it gives schools the chance to 
generate the revenue necessary to preserve scholarships and other 
opportunities.
  We can compensate athletes without abandoning what makes college 
sports unique. We can give players freedom without accepting chaos. And 
we can

[[Page S5030]]

preserve college sports for the next generation. Congress should act. 
Here is our moment. The clock is ticking. Game on.
  I yield the floor.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from New Jersey.
  Mr. BOOKER. Madam President, I am so grateful to be recognized. It is 
amazing how for more than 14 years in this place, I was trying to get 
Congress to step in to do something to help athletes, but the answer 
was no, no, no. But suddenly, when college athletes start winning cases 
in court against a $20 billion gravy train that has been benefiting 
everybody but college athletes, suddenly there seems to be this race to 
pass a bill to allegedly protect college athletes. But really, we know 
who this bill is protecting.
  The Senate is on the verge--it really could happen tonight--of 
passing a bill that could reshape college sports for generations to 
come. It is a bill that was written without input from college athletes 
themselves, whose lives will be directly affected. It violates a simple 
rule: nothing about us without us.
  Well, this is a bill, going forward, where college athletes and the 
organizations that represent them have not had a seat at the table. It 
is a bill that was actually shaped by millions of dollars in lobbying 
from the NCAA and other powerful interests that control that industry. 
And at its core, it is a bill that undermines the rights of young 
people who pour their blood, sweat, tears, countless hours of work, and 
more into their craft. Their voices should have shaped this debate. We 
should have listened to their wisdom before voting today, before 
rushing to this vote as the last thing we do before a long recess.
  Plain and simple, athletes produce this wealth, produce these 
opportunities, create this industry, and they should have been at the 
table, but they weren't given that chance. So, today, I am going to do 
my best to elevate the concerns of college athletes, the concerns that 
over this last decade they have shared with me, concerns that I have 
learned myself because I love college sports.
  In an increasingly divided country, college sports is one of the few 
things that actually bring us together. There is something special 
about college sports, and U.S. college sports--heck, there is something 
uniquely American about them. I was lucky enough to be a part of that. 
I played Division I football at Stanford. I know firsthand what 
athletes sacrifice. I know what the system too often denies them in 
return. That is why I have spent my entire Senate career fighting for 
justice for college athletes.

  This is not a new issue for me. Back in 2014, I said to the then-
president of the NCAA when he testified before Congress--very bluntly, 
I told him:

       [I]t is exploitation when you have an athlete working 60 to 
     70 hours a week but [yet still] not . . . able to afford the 
     basic necessities.
       If [an athlete is] going into their own pocket after giving 
     up their knees . . . to make millions of dollars for the 
     university and [those universities aren't] even compensating 
     them appropriately, that . . . is exploitation of a college 
     athlete.

  That was me back in 2014 to the then-head of the NCAA.
  Twelve years later, athletes have won important new rights through 
their own tireless advocacy at State legislatures, in courts, but now 
what do we see? We see that the NCAA's desire for control has not 
subsided. In fact, it has grown. The NCAA has spent years now pursuing 
a legislative strategy here in Congress to cement their control over 
athletes, to put it into Federal law that they alone have the power and 
the control over those athletes.
  With this bill we are about to do--should it be passed in the House 
and signed by the President--it is a bill for the control and power of 
the NCAA and member institutions. It is not about athletes, who are 
winning their rights, winning court cases, gaining leverage, and now 
are having that taken away from them.
  It is important to understand just how big of a business this is. 
This is why we see commercials during games we are watching, millions 
of dollars of lobbyists--because this is a $20 billion industry. Today, 
more than 40 college football coaches make more than $5 million a year. 
In most States, it is the highest paid State employee. In 2025 alone, 
15 fired coaches secured buyouts worth nearly a quarter of a billion 
dollars. That is $250 million paid to coaches not to coach.
  You hear people running around here lobbying, saying: Oh, we have a 
financial crisis in college sports. Nobody is trying to deal with that 
issue.
  And now we see the TV deals growing bigger and bigger and bigger. The 
SEC has a deal worth about $3 billion over 10 years. The Big Ten's deal 
is worth $7 billion over 7 years. The wealth and money are growing. 
That is why the lobbyists and millionaires were down here lobbying. 
This is big business, folks. It has been big business for years.
  What has changed is that the athletes were finally beginning to be 
able to have the fight for the share in the value that they create. 
They have developed over recent years more power and more leverage, and 
that is a scary thing for the NCAA.
  I want to be honest about one point--about who creates a 
disproportionate amount of that value. That is actually Black athletes. 
They have an outsize role in building college sports into the economic 
powerhouse it is because they are overrepresented in the revenue-
driving sports. Think about this: In men's basketball, Black Americans 
are 44 percent of the players. In football, it is 40 percent of the 
players. They are overrepresented in these powerful, money-generating 
sports. For decades, these athletes were shut out from sharing in the 
value they created, the billions of dollars of revenue they created. 
They were shut out of it.
  So this is not just a college sports issue; it is an economic justice 
issue and a civil rights issue. Any effort to limit athletes' ability 
to earn will fall especially hard on Black athletes and many athletes 
of different backgrounds, and that is why the voices of athletes are so 
critical. That is why the diverse voices of athletes are so critical 
and why civil rights leaders have been condemning this bill.
  Listen to Congresswoman Yvette Clarke, who is the chair of the 
Congressional Black Caucus. She said:

       Black athletes have transformed college athletics into one 
     of the most successful enterprises in America. Yet the people 
     most affected by this legislation have not been meaningfully 
     included in shaping it.
       No legislation that disproportionately affects Black 
     athletes should move through Congress without meaningful 
     participation from the communities it will most directly 
     affect.

  Or listen to Derrick Johnson, the President of the NAACP. President 
Johnson said:

       The so-called Protect College Sports Act does little to 
     actually protect the student athletes who make this 
     multibillion-dollar industry possible. Instead, it shields 
     powerful institutions from accountability while asking Black 
     athletes to surrender their rights, their leverage and their 
     voice.

  So now, just as athletes all across America are finally starting to 
share in the value they are creating, they are starting to finally get 
voices that are recognized by courts and State legislatures, we are 
told that Congress must immediately step in, 30-plus days from an 
election, to save college sports. But who are we saving it from, and 
what are we saving it for? The answer to those questions is actually in 
the text of the bill, to know what this is really about. Let me walk 
through some of my specific concerns that point to the hypocrisy here.
  First, this bill would make it harder for athletes to sign NIL deals. 
Section 101 relates to athletes' NIL rights, but it is important to 
remember athletes already have NIL rights. They fought for them. They 
earned them. They won them in court and State legislatures. What this 
section actually does is add new layers of review, redtape, 
bureaucracy, and uncertainty between athletes and the opportunities 
before them.
  Second, this bill limits athletes' ability to seek justice in court. 
Section 118 gives the NCAA sweeping immunity from antitrust liability--
a sweeping immunity we scarcely have ever given any other industry.
  For decades, the NCAA used its market power to restrict athletes' 
rights, and then it lost in court again and again.
  It is so exceedingly rare for Congress to grant exemptions from the 
Nation's antitrust laws, and it is rarer still for Congress to do so 
for an organization with the NCAA's track record of antitrust 
violations.

[[Page S5031]]

  The NCAA has violated antitrust laws and the trust of college 
athletes over and over again, and yet they are the big winners here. 
This bill would permanently shield the NCAA from the kind of liability 
in these key areas--a permanent shield. They don't have to come back to 
Congress in 10 years, 15 years, 25 years; it is permanent. This means 
less accountability for the NCAA and fewer tools for athletes to
