challenge the NCAA's abuses of power when they have an incredible track 
record of abusing their power.
  Third, this bill puts the burden of enforcement on athletes. Think 
about this: Section 119 of the bill creates a private right of action 
for athletes. It is the sole enforcement mechanism in this bill.
  There is no role for States' attorneys general, no role for the 
Federal Trade Commission, no role for anyone. The sole enforcement 
mechanism is on the student-athletes themselves to somehow sue 
powerful, powerful entities.
  A young athlete whose rights are violated would have to enforce their 
own rights. So let's play this out for a second. Put yourself in the 
position of one of these young athletes, teenager. Say you are 18 years 
old. You are new on campus. You have worked your entire life to find 
this opportunity. Maybe you are from a low-income community.
  You might not come from a lot of money, but you realized your dream 
to play ball at a collegiate level. And in a few weeks, something 
terrible happens. Your coach has violated your rights in some way. 
Maybe they demanded you play through a serious injury, leading to 
permanent injury. Maybe they sexually harassed you. Maybe they kept you 
on a practice field in 110-degree heat with 90 percent humidity without 
a water break.
  This 18-year-old knows something is wrong. They know their rights are 
being violated, but under this bill, they would be on their own to seek 
justice. They would have to file a lawsuit against their coach, the 
person who controls their playing time, their roster slot; the person 
who controls their future.
  Can you imagine that ever happening? Well, it rarely would. It is 
fundamentally unfair to put all of this on the college athlete and for 
this body to hand over permanent antitrust exemptions and give no 
enforcement mechanism than to trust the institution that has been 
violating athletes' rights for decades.
  We are basically telling teenagers and young people in sports: You 
need to come up with the time, the money, the lawyers to challenge the 
person who has violated your rights, the same person who happens to 
control your destiny.
  It is not a serious way to make sure this bill is properly enforced. 
Congress puts independent groups together or assigns it to oversight 
committees like the FTC to enforce rules all over this country. Why not 
now?
  Fourth, this bill undermines States' ability to protect college 
athletes' rights. Section 121 preempts certain State-level laws related 
to civil rights, privacy, and data breaches. That means, if an 
athlete's civil rights are violated, if their health records are leaked 
online, or their personal information is somehow misused by their 
conference or the university, the athlete will have fewer legal 
protections and fewer options available for them to seek justice.
  How can we have a nation that is basically saying: We are not just 
going to put the NCAA in charge of your lives, but we are going to take 
away your ability to seek legal redress?
  Fifth, this bill offers only temporary protections for women's and 
Olympic sports while permanent antitrust exemptions for the NCAA. The 
sponsors of this bill say it will protect women's and Olympic sports, 
but section 125 has a sunset.
  The protections expire in 4 to 9 years, depending on the size of the 
program. The NCAA's antitrust exemption is permanent. The protections 
for women's and Olympic sports are only temporary. And athletes are 
noticing.
  Listen to Caitlin VanderKlok, a cross-country athlete at the 
University of Tennessee. She says:
  Commercials are claiming that the Protect College Sports Act will 
protect women and Olympic sport athletes like me. But simply mentioning 
women's and Olympic sports in a bill does not mean those athletes are 
actually protected, she writes. Even where protections apply, they 
expire, and athletes are not given a right under this bill to go to 
court and enforce them. If we are going to tell athletes that this bill 
protects their opportunities, we should also tell them the facts.
  Well, Caitlin, you are right. People are lying to you. People in this 
body that say it protects women's and Olympic sports are lying to you 
by not telling you the whole truth; that those protections expire.
  Listen to Meghann Burke, executive director of the National Women's 
Soccer League Players Association.
  She says:
  There is a profound contradiction at the center of this legislation: 
the claim that the Protect College Sports Act protects women in sports. 
In reality, the bill risks undermining the promise of title IX. Schools 
should not be able to evade their title IX obligations simply by 
calling revenue-sharing payments compensation rather than athletic 
benefits. At a time when college athletes are beginning to exercise 
their collective power, we should not be putting new barriers in their 
path toward collective bargaining.
  Listen to college athletes. Listen to the people that represent 
college athletes. Listen to the people that represent athletes who have 
come out of college sports and are now playing in the pros.
  But this is a bill not about college athletes; this is a bill about 
wealthy people, wealthy coaches, wealthy institutions, and an NCAA that 
has enjoyed unbridled power for decades as we watched discrimination, 
hardships, injustice. And finally--finally--athletes started getting 
some power in the equation.
  And this body--after my trying for 10 to 15 years to get them to step 
in and help college athletes, this body rushes in to suddenly say: We 
are protecting college athletes.
  Well, that is not true.
  So, look, I know how things work here. I have offered some ways to 
try to improve this bill. I and colleagues Murphy and Blumenthal and 
others offered a series of amendments built on a simple principle: If 
we are going to impose rules on athletes, we should apply the same 
rules to coaches.
  Take compensation, this bill caps what schools can share directly 
with athletes. So we offered an amendment to cap what schools can pay 
coaches to $5 million a year. That is still very good money.
  This amendment is not going to get a vote on the floor today.
  Take endorsement deals, the bill subjects athletes' NIL deals to 
fair-market value review, a valid business purpose standard, approval, 
and disclosure. So we offered an amendment to require that same process 
for coaches' deals, for their endorsement deals.
  Well, we are not going to be voting on that amendment because it is 
not about the athletes, it is not about fairness; it is enforcing 
hypocrisy.
  Let's take transfers, which I have heard already some of my 
colleagues talk about on the floor. This bill allows an athlete to 
transfer one time. If they transfer again, they have to sit out a year.
  Well, reasonable people could disagree on that policy, but if you are 
going to restrict athletes' ability to move between schools, the same 
rules should apply to coaches. We offered an amendment to make that 
happen. It was not accepted.
  Imagine you are that athlete, promised by coaches everything, and 
that coach gets to leave and go to as many schools as they want, but 
you are stuck.
  Let's take the buyouts. Do you remember the 15 fired coaches and 
their buyouts being worth--this last year--a quarter of a billion 
dollars? We offered an amendment to simply cap future buyouts at 1-
year's salary. This would protect schools across the country from being 
forced to plunge millions of dollars into college coaches who are not 
even coaching.
  It would give them the opportunity to not have to pay these 
exorbitant prices when they could be investing in things to improve 
education of all kids.
  Well, that amendment is not going to get a vote today. Common sense.
  Take women's Olympic sports. I offered a simple amendment to strike 
the sunset provision and make those provisions permanent just like the 
NCAA's antitrust exemption.

[[Page S5032]]

  Hey, you are going to give permanent protection to the wealthy and 
the powerful, then protect women's sports and Olympic sports where our 
Olympic athletes come out of pocket all their lives, trying to get into 
the Olympics to represent the United States of America. If this is 
about patriotism, if this is about our Nation, then protect Olympic 
sports. Don't let that provision expire.
  Well, that is not going to get a vote today. The hypocrisy in this 
bill, the refusal to accept amendments exposes that hypocrisy.
  And, finally, this is the most insulting part of the bill. Take 
HBCUs, the sponsors have been putting people on TV to talk about how 
this does so much for HBCUs. They point to an authorization of funding, 
but an authorization guarantees nothing, not a single penny.
  Senators Warnock, Alsobrooks, Blunt Rochester, Murphy, and I offered 
a simple amendment: turn that authorization into an appropriation. If 
you are going to parade Black people in your millions of dollars' worth 
of ads, saying you are giving money to HBCUs, maybe the text of this 
bill should actually give money to HBCUs. Let's deliver on that promise 
today. Let's actually cut a check to historically Black colleges and 
universities.
  But no, no. This bill says we should deal with a promise, an 
intention, and this amendment is not going to be voted on.
  I am not alone in my concerns about this bill. There are so many 
people in this Nation--who stand up for the underdog, who stand up for 
the little guy and the little gal, who represent athletes or fight for 
equal rights--that object to this bill.
  I want to bring the concerns of more of those people into this 
Chamber and make sure it is a part of the official Record.
  Listen to a group of athletes who survived sexual assault and parents 
who lost children during college sports activities. They wrote a 
powerful letter pleading with Senators. Here is an excerpt of their 
letter:
  These are not abstract policy concerns to us. We know firsthand the 
consequences when institutions are allowed to police themselves, when 
reports of abuse or dangerous practices remain inside the institution, 
and when victims are left without meaningful accountability after harm 
is done.
  They continue:
  Universities, conferences, and other powerful college sports' 
interests have had extensive opportunities to make their case as this 
legislation has developed. Before Congress grants sweeping new 
protections to the college sports system, we ask that you hear directly 
from those of us who have experienced the consequences when that system 
fails athletes.
  Is anybody listening? Have we had them at the hearings? Have their 
voices been taken into account?
  Listen to some of our labor leaders, listen to the AFL-CIO as they 
write:
  The Protect College Sports Act is not a compromise between competing 
interests but a legislative effort that preserves institutional control 
at a time when courts, lawmakers, and the public are increasingly 
questioning the fairness of the existing model. The bill gained its 
current momentum not when athletes' interests were satisfied but when 
the richest intercollegiate conferences in the country voted to endorse 
the bill. That should tell you everything you need to know about who 
this bill protects.
  Listen to former senior Justice Department officials, including a 
former principal Deputy Assistant Attorney General, a former Acting 
Associate Attorney General, and a former Acting Assistant Attorney 
General for the Antitrust Division. Listen to what they write:

       The Protect College Sports Act includes a broad antitrust 
     exemption that would immunize the NCAA from any Federal or 
     State enforcement for weaponizing its enormous power over 
     college athletes to cap pay, limit eligibility and transfers, 
     or otherwise impose burdens or restrictions on players.

       This would constitute the most sweeping antitrust exemption 
     granted by Congress since the McCarran-Ferguson Act of 1945.

  Not since 1945 have we seen what Congress is about to do today.
  These former attorneys general continue:

       Traditionally, antitrust immunity is granted only when the 
     parties agree to rules through a collective bargaining 
     process--and only for so long as a bargaining relationship is 
     in place--or when rules are conditioned on state or federal 
     regulatory oversight. However, neither protection is included 
     in the [Protect College Sports Act].

  In other words, there is no oversight from the Federal Government. 
The only hope athletes have is to sue.
  The letter continues:

       As currently conceived, this is not a bill that protects 
     college sports. It is a bill that protects the NCAA's ability 
     to abuse its power over college athletes. A vote for the 
     PCSA--

  They write--

     is a vote against college athletes. It tells them their 
     rights are trivial when a billion-dollar entity like the NCAA 
     starts making demands.

  And listen to the athletes' own organizations.
  Here is athletes.org:

     The [Protect College Sports Act] protects schools, not 
     athletes. It rolls back athletes' hard-earned rights while 
     protecting the financial interests of the NCAA, its member 
     conferences, universities, and--

  I add ``well-paid''--

     coaches. Worse, many of its proposed solutions fail to 
     address the problems they claim to solve, risking even more 
     confusion and instability across college sports.

  The PRESIDING OFFICER (Ms. Ernst). The Senator's time is expired.
  Mr. BOOKER. I request unanimous consent for 5 more minutes to 
conclude my remarks.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Washington.
  Ms. CANTWELL. Reserving the right to object, can we modify that and 
ask for an additional 20 minutes so the Senator from Texas and I can 
also be heard?
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. Does the Senator so modify his request?
  Mr. BOOKER. Absolutely.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. Without objection, it is so ordered.
  Mr. BOOKER. Here is the National College Players Association:

       This bill is clearly not ready for a vote. Congress should 
     stop rushing it and fully vet the problems before asking 
     members to vote.

  I could go on with all of the compelling, painful letters that have 
been written.
  This is the wrong focus for Congress 30 days before midterm 
elections.
  There is one more question we should be asking about before we take 
this vote: Is this really what we should be spending our time on? Our 
country faces real challenges. Gas is at $5. Diesel costs are at a 
record high. We are at war abroad. Healthcare expenses are 
skyrocketing. And the U.S. Senate is spending precious time on the 
floor rewriting the rules of college sports while athletes, athletes 
groups, survivors of sexual assault, labor, and civil rights leaders 
are all begging Congress to slow down and listen to their voices. What 
does this say about our priorities?
  It is also worth noting that many of my Senators are supporting this 
bill. They like to preach the gospel of small government. They talk 
about the love of free markets and small government; the importance of 
freedom and America's right to pursue the opportunities that are best 
for them. But there seems to be a major exception to that kind of 
philosophy when it comes to young people who are disproportionately 
Black. There seems to be this high-and-mighty rhetoric about freedom 
and free markets when it comes to every other area of our economy but 
not this $20 billion industry.
  Under this Federal bill, the Federal Government will decide how many 
schools can join an athletic conference, when certain games can be 
played, and how much revenue athletes can receive from their own 
schools. This is a massive amount of market intervention. Where else 
does the Federal Government step in to limit what people can earn while 
allowing the institutions that control the market to pay their 
executives anything they want? There is not one single industry.
  My playing days are long over--that is obvious--but I think about 
athletes that are playing today and former athletes who have been 
calling me about their pain, about their suffering, about their 
reaching in their own pockets to pay for medical costs, who see that 
this bill's medical pool is inadequate to the rising costs of CTE and 
more.
  I am thinking hard about athletes who are going to come after them 
who are joining this institution of NCAA with such a long history of 
exploitation and violating people's rights.

[[Page S5033]]

  Young people who work 40, 50 hours, dedicate themselves to their 
craft; young people who literally put their bodies on the line; young 
people whose talent generates billions of dollars for others--they 
deserve a voice in the decisions that are determining their future.
  They deserve a system that does more to protect their rights than 
saying: It is all on you. Sue if you want to. Sue your coach.
  They deserve to know that when they stand up and demand a fairer 
system, when they win in the courts and State legislatures, this body 
won't betray them by pulling the rug out from underneath them.
  They deserve leaders in this body who will stand with the athletes 
first.
  We may lose this vote today, but athletes know how to come back from 
a loss. They know how to get back up and go to work. They know how to 
keep fighting for what they believe is right.
  I am going to keep fighting alongside these athletes. I am going to 
keep fighting against an institution called the NCAA that has violated 
their rights all throughout my lifetime.
  I urge my colleagues to oppose this bill. I urge my colleagues to 
stand up for justice. I urge my colleagues not to race to get a bill 
done right before an election. I urge my colleagues to hold hearings 
and actually listen to survivors of sexual assault, parents of dead 
college athletes, to the kids who have serious, debilitating injuries 
right now and are paying their own medical bills. I beg my colleagues 
not to do this. It is wrong. The next Congress would give us time. The 
next Congress would give us a chance.
  But, no, billionaires and millionaires and lobbyists and funding has 
poured into this place, and suddenly, right before we leave, the last 
thing we are doing is a bill that is going to hurt so many.
  I regret this day. I regret watching my colleagues do this. But the 
fight continues.
  I yield the floor.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Texas.
  Mr. CRUZ. Madam President, the Senate will soon pass landmark 
legislation to protect college sports. When any legislation, 
particularly landmark legislation, is enacted into law, there can be 
debate over that law's implementation. This isn't surprising. As the 
Senate prepares to potentially send the PCSA to the House of 
Representatives, I want to take a few moments to briefly explain the 
reasoning and intent behind some select, key provisions of the PCSA. I 
believe this will be useful not only for my colleagues here today, but 
for the PCSA's implementation.
  Section 118 of the PCSA provides specific, targeted, and narrow 
antitrust protection for intercollegiate athletic associations, such as 
the NCAA, conferences, institutions, and the designated enforcement 
entity within the House settlement--the College Sports Commission. 
Although these entities are composed of schools--or conferences--who 
have chosen voluntarily to adhere to certain rules as a condition of 
membership, these rules cannot be enforced due to constant litigation 
and rules set by ``court order.''
  Section 118 intends to end this by providing antitrust liability when 
entities act, including through use of their own rules or bylaws, to 
enforce or comply with specified sections of the PCSA.
  The intended antitrust protection is comprehensive for all antitrust 
suits, including State statutory or common laws governing antitrust, 
trade regulation, trade restraints, and unfair methods of competition.
  The PCSA intends to stop the current instability and close any 
further avenues for litigants to use antitrust claims to undermine the 
requirements and standards specified in the PCSA.
  The PCSA intends the revenue share cap adopted in the House 
settlement to be a ``hard cap'' without any ``loopholes.''
  It is no secret that institutions currently take advantage of a gray 
area in the settlement that allows them to directly use, contract with, 
or maintain any a relationship, even an indirect one, with an 
``associated entity.'' Today, an associated entity, which is oftentimes 
a collective, booster, a multimedia rights company--MMR--redirects 
compensation to student athletes that would have otherwise gone to the 
athletic department.
  By funneling these arrangements through an ``associated entity,'' an 
institution can spend money or recruit in a way that would otherwise 
exceed or circumvent the House settlement's revenue share limits. The 
PCSA intends to clarify this ambiguity and ensure an even playing 
field.
  We further considered the ongoing debate in the courts around whether 
MMRs or collectives should constitute associated entities. There has 
yet to be case, and likely never will be one, when an MMR is anything 
but an ``associated entity.''
  The PCSA adopts the settlement's definition of ``associated entity'' 
and also expressly states that collectives are ``associated entities.'' 
As introduced, the PCSA included text to clarify that an entity was 
``associated'' if they ``acted for the benefit of, in coordination 
with, or at the direction of the institution.''
  This was discussed within the College Sports Commission's guidance 
and practice, but we ended up removing that phrase from the definition 
to ensure consistent definitions for ease of compliance.
  That decision should not be construed as affirming that an entity 
that acts in this manner should not be considered ``associated.'' We 
instead decided that this CSC guidance and practice was upheld by the 
Special Master and subsequently by a Federal court.
  We understood the existing CSC guidance and practice to inform the 
application of the term ``associated entity'' and that deals involving 
MMRs, corporate sponsors, or any third party will be scrutinized to 
ensure there is no effort to elude being considered an associated 
entity. In fact, we have added legally-enforceable certification 
requirements by schools, vendors, and MMRs, when applicable, to uphold 
the cap and prevent circumvention.
  The PCSA recognizes, protects, and codifies portions of the House 
settlement, particularly with respect to the revenue share cap.
  But there are several provisions--specifically in section 114--where 
we have chosen a different Federal requirement than in the settlement.
  For example, in section 114, the PCSA:

       1. Counts associated entity compensation towards the rev 
     share cap,
       2. Creates a retention fund that allows schools to exceed 
     the rev share cap with certain requirements,
       3. Requires MMR and other certain corporate sponsors to 
     provide certifications,
       4. Requires all NIL deals--including third party deals--to 
     be subject to a valid business purpose test.

  We do not want the PCSA to terminate the House settlement. We fully 
expect that the settlement will remain in effect. We instead chose to 
enact Federal requirements to govern when the PCSA establishes a 
different requirement than what is in the settlement.
  Thus, the PCSA assumes the continuation of the settlement--even 
providing antitrust protections for its ongoing implementation--while 
providing Federal requirements that governs when there are conflicting 
provisions within the settlement.
  To stop an institution's ``redirection'' of funds to a student 
athlete, the PCSA imposes additional requirements on all NIL agreements 
involving an institution's multimedia rights holder or certain entities 
with which the institution has a commercial relationship.
  In such deals, the institution and third-party entity must each sign 
a certification attesting that the institution was not involved in 
sourcing, negotiating, or paying for the NIL agreement. Any applicable 
deal subject to this section that fails to comply with these 
certification requirements violates Federal law.
  These certification requirements are supplemental to all other 
provisions of the PCSA. In other words, even if the certifications are 
provided, there still must be an independent determination--likely by 
the College Sports Commission--if the deal involves an associated 
entity and an evaluation of the proposed deal.
  Such certification may be relevant to the College Sports Commission's 
determination of whether the entity is associated, but is not 
dispositive of the question. The certification is intended to 
supplement and aid the College Sports Commission's review process, as 
well as ensure compliance with the PCSA.

[[Page S5034]]

  Section 118(d) of the PCSA allows an intercollegiate athletic 
association or conference to designate an entity under its control 
within the House settlement, for which it won't be unlawful under the 
antitrust laws, to enforce or comply with section 114.
  The College Sports Commission has been designated as the enforcement 
entity under the settlement and any rulemaking, guidance, investigative 
or enforcement action, or penalty issued by the College Sports 
Commission for purposes of section 114 is intended to be protected from 
liability under the antitrust laws.
  Section 119(e) is intended to preserve the arbitration framework 
created by the settlement. Any dispute a student athlete, institution, 
or associated entity or individual may have regarding any CSC 
determination must be arbitrated consistent with the terms of the 
settlement.
  Section 121(a) preempts any law, regulation, rule, requirement, or 
standard that conflicts with the PCSA, including the established 
arbitration framework.
  With some exceptions, section 123 prohibits the PCSA from applying to 
an action or proceeding commenced prior to the enactment of the PCSA.
  The purpose of this section is to preserve most ``pending'' active 
legal cases that were commenced prior to enactment of the PCSA.
  There are three notable statutory exceptions:
  First, section 123(a) says the PCSA shall apply to pending cases 
dealing with sports gambling or wagering matters.
  Second, section 123(b)(2) specifies a ``pending case'' cannot allow a 
student athlete eligibility to compete or participate on a D1 or D2 
sports team for more than 5 total years.
  We do not want the PCSA's eligibility provisions to be undermined by 
court orders that directly conflict with the implementation of the 
PCSA's eligibility provisions.
  Third, section 123(b)(2) does not permit a ``pending case'' to 
undermine the revenue share cap.
  The PCSA's modifications to the House settlement, as well as the 
implementation of the broader settlement framework contemplated in this 
bill, are critical to its success at stabilizing college sports and 
ending the legal chaos.
  We do not want to provide any avenue for judicial decisions to 
prohibit enforcement of the revenue share cap or prohibit modifications 
to the settlement when the PCSA governs a specific requirement.
  This exception intends to prevent that type of conclusion to be drawn 
by a court because it would defeat a critical purpose for why Congress 
passed the PCSA.
  I would like to briefly discuss how the PCSA's eligibility standards 
relate to the PCSA's preemption in section 121.
  Section 113 specifies eligibility standards for individuals to 
participate in NCAA D1 or D2 college athletics.
  Per section 118, if the NCAA, conferences, or institutions enforce or 
comply with these provisions, they will receive antitrust immunity.
  Section 113 species five eligibility standards:

       You have to be a student athlete.
       You have to meet academic standards.
       You cannot be a professional athlete.
       You have to comply with established rules that restrict 
     eligibility for violating Federal or State law.
       You can only play for 5 years maximum and cannot play past 
     the age of 24.

  The NCAA, conferences, and institutions are free to place additional 
eligibility restrictions not specified in the PCSA, but those 
restrictions will not receive Federal antitrust protections unless 
otherwise permitted.
  Establishing such additional eligibility restrictions is also not a 
violation of section 113.
  Similarly, States may impose additional eligibility requirements not 
expressly prohibited by section 121.
  The PCSA has two types of preemption:

       1. ``Conflict Preemption''--A State law cannot ``conflict 
     with any provision of this title that would prevent 
     compliance with this title''; and
       2. ``Field Preemption''--related to NIL compensation 
     matters, transfer, and subsections (a)(1)-(3) or (b) of 
     section 113.

  Those specified eligibility categories are: No. 1, you have to be a 
student athlete; No. 2, you can't be a professional athlete; No. 3, you 
have to meet academic standards; and No. 4, you have to abide by the 
age restrictions and maximum number of years to play.
  We made a deliberate drafting choice to only have ``field 
preemption'' for subsections (a)(1)-(3) or (b) to ensure that States 
can legislate on eligibility matters not covered in the PCSA, provided 
that those laws also don't ``conflict'' with the PCSA.
  We intend that State laws that prohibit men from competing in women's 
sports are clearly not preempted by section 121 because they neither 
conflict with the PCSA nor do they relate to those matters specified in 
subsection (a)(1)-(3) or (b) of section 113.
  The PCSA does not contain any express waiver of any sovereign 
immunity rights of a public institution, nor is it intended to contain 
any express or implied waiver of the sovereign immunity rights of a 
public institution.
  Title II amends the Sports Broadcasting Act to allow for a 
``covered'' entity to pool media rights and not be subject to antitrust 
liability.
  In order to receive this protection, the entity must meet the 
membership requirements specified in the bill and other requirements 
expressly outlined in the text.
  A conference or institution's decisions to join or participate in 
this pooled media entity is completely voluntary and optional.
  We have made clear that they cannot be held liable for a decision not 
to join the entity nor can they be forced to join or participate in the 
entity.
  Moreover, this title makes clear that it does impact any existing 
contracts.
  Madam President, today, the Senate has the opportunity to protect 
more than half a million college student-athletes and the millions more 
who will follow in their footsteps.
  The era of NIL is here, and it is not going away. Yet the system we 
have today is unsustainable, plain and simple. Students are 
transferring three and four times. Pros are trying to play ball against 
college kids. Conflicting court decisions have created a fragmented 
system--one that no one intended. Young men and women are losing 
opportunities to compete and receive an education.
  The question before this body is whether we will act or stand by 
while the institution that Americans have known and loved for 
generations--college sports--is fundamentally changed.
  Will we protect the future of women's sports, of Olympic sports, of 
nonrevenue sports, or allow college athletics to be consumed by 
uncontrollable bidding wars that force schools to cut programs simply 
because they don't generate headlines or massive television contracts? 
The answer to this question should be simple: We must act, and we must 
act now. We must pass the bipartisan Protect College Sports Act.
  This bill puts an end to the chaos plaguing college athletics. It 
protects NIL opportunities because student-athletes deserve the 
opportunity to benefit from their talent without being taken advantage 
of by unscrupulous agents and backdoor deals. It creates clear, 
enforceable rules for eligibility and transfers. It protects our 
traditions and rivalries. It prevents the most powerful and wealthy 
conferences from breaking away to form a superleague.
  But most importantly, it protects our student-athletes. It ensures 
that the men's volleyball player has a team to come back to next year. 
It ensures that a first-generation student who excels in track and 
field has the opportunity to receive an education. It ensures that when 
American athletes compete on the Olympic stage, it is the United States 
and not China and not Russia bringing home the gold. It preserves the 
traditions passed down from one generation to the next--the second 
graders learning to cheer for the Aggies, just as their parents and 
grandparents did before them, with the hope that when they get to 
college, the program they grew up loving will still be there. This bill 
is about protecting what makes college sports uniquely American.
  The Protect College Sports Act has an amazing coalition of support. 
It has the support of 32 conferences--every single major conference--as 
well as more than 380 schools and also historically Black colleges 
across all 50 States and Washington, DC. It has the support of the NFL, 
the NBA, Major League Baseball, the National Hockey

[[Page S5035]]

League, the U.S. Olympic and Paralympic Committee, and more than 
500,000 student-athletes across the country. Don't take my word for it; 
take their word for it. They are the ones on the frontlines directly 
impacted by the chaos unfolding.
  This bill isn't perfect. No bill ever is. What this bill reflects is 
a compromise--tough conversations and Republicans and Democrats sitting 
across the table from one another, working through disagreements, and 
producing legislation that can actually get across the finish line.
  I want to take a moment to express my sincere gratitude to my staff 
and to the staff of Ranking Member Cantwell who have devoted thousands 
of hours to working on this bill.
  From Ranking Member Cantwell's committee and personal office staff, I 
would like to thank Lila Helms, Melissa Porter, Nicole Frazer, Phil 
Murphy, Ramesh Nagarajan, Erik Eykel, Kate Sprague, Ciara O'Donoghue, 
Josie Lang, Tricia Enright, Rob Blumenthal, Seth Kolloen, Elizabeth 
Cuttner, Amit Ronen, Ansley Lacitis, Lauren Bazel, and Shannon Smith.
  I would also like to thank from my team Brad Grantz, Joel Wellum, 
Blair Taylor, Robert Smith, Mike Spierto, Ryan Cannon, Bethany Holden, 
Rachel Cusnir, Christina Herrera, Dan Huguenin, and Nicole Christus 
from the Commerce Committee and John Etue, Preston Howey, Omri Ceren, 
Macarena Martinez, and Chavonne Ludick from my personal office. This 
team is extraordinary, and I cannot thank them all enough for their 
incredibly hard work day in and day out.
  I would also like to extend my deepest thanks to my staff director on 
the committee, Brad Grantz, and to my policy director for consumer 
protection, Joel Wellum. Brad and Joel both joined the committee when I 
became ranking member and began working on this issue right away. They 
have been dedicated for the past 3 years to resolving the chaos that is 
college sports. I also want to thank their wives Katie and Stephanie 
for enduring the many hours away from the family so they can pass this 
landmark legislation.
  In the course of this bill, I have used more than a few football 
analogies, so I am going to switch it up tonight.
  Picture March Madness. A Cinderella team that few thought would make 
it past the opening round is facing the No. 1 seed. The clock is 
winding down. The underdog is down by 2 points with 1 second left. The 
ball goes up. A 3-pointer. The buzzer sounds. The crowd erupts.
  That Cinderella story is what the Protect College Sports Act is 
trying to preserve.
  It is about protecting the underdog and ensuring that each and every 
team has the opportunity to develop and compete and maybe even make it 
to the Final Four next season, but those stories only happen if those 
teams still exist.
  So I urge my colleagues: Let's show the American people that 
bipartisanship still exists. Let's show them that Republicans and 
Democrats can still come together to solve a problem facing this 
country.
  Let's protect millions of student-athletes. Let's protect the 
traditions. Let's protect the future of college sports. Let's pass the 
Protect College Sports Act, and let's get a big bipartisan vote in this 
body to show that the Senate can still make a difference in America.
  I yield the floor.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The Senator from Washington.
  Ms. CANTWELL. Madam President, I know the floor has been busy this 
afternoon with people discussing the Protect College Sports Act, and I 
want to thank all my colleagues for participating in a debate.
  That is what this institution is supposed to be about. It is supposed 
to be about an amendment process, and it is supposed to be about a 
product that comes out of committee, and it is supposed to be about our 
colleagues showing up. So I want to thank my colleagues, particularly 
Senator Booker and Senator Murphy, who came to the floor to express 
their views on this bill.
  I want to thank both of them. You know, I certainly agree with 
Senator Murphy on his long-standing effort to think about collective 
bargaining in college sports. He has been very outspoken on that for 
many years. And I want to thank Senator Booker for working with me for 
many, many years on this issue, and that doesn't mean that he still 
doesn't have reservations, but it means that he is committed to this 
issue writ large.
  But we do see a different conclusion here tonight. And I want to 
thank my colleagues for passing a bill out of committee and now getting 
it to this threshold because, unlike my colleagues' comments before, 
there are several things in this bill that are really important to 
enshrine into Federal law at this time.
  First and foremost, the Federal right to NIL and making sure that it 
is not State laws that have been quite onerous on athletes--that is, 
laws that say if you conflict with your university, your university 
wins and if you conflict with other issues, you don't get NIL. So 
creating a Federal standard is very important.
  But compensation on revenue sharing is probably the most important 
thing we could possibly enshrine in Federal law today. This is saying, 
for the first time, that an athlete gets a percentage of the media 
rights, the ticket sales, and the sponsorship.
  That has not been said before in a court case decision. It was agreed 
to in a settlement agreement--in a settlement agreement called Grant 
House v. NCAA. Now, that settlement agreement has had its ups and 
downs, and I hope it prevails, but I know this: We can put into Federal 
law that enshrinement so that the Senate is clear.
  My colleagues have said: Why do this now? Well, because there was a 
difference of opinion, and even Justice Brett Kavanaugh, in the 
decision of NCAA v. Alston by the Supreme Court, said: Yes, I think 
athletes deserve a recognition of revenue, but Congress could step in 
and change that.
  And my colleagues may know that the House of Representatives did try 
to change that. The House of Representatives did try to pass a bill 
that said: No, they don't deserve revenue sharing. They don't deserve 
revenue sharing. That is the bill the House came very close to passing.
  And so tonight, we are taking a monumental step that I agree that 
maybe not all 100 people agree on, but I think the majority of our 
colleagues agree on these two principles that the Supreme Court also 
has spoken to, but that we are now putting in a Federal law because, as 
Brett Kavanaugh said, Congress could change its mind. Congress could do 
something differently. But tonight, we are speaking. We believe in 
these two things.
  We also believe in a health and safety standard in Federal law, one 
that protects the athletes beyond the way the law works today and gives 
them the right to sue and hold these people accountable for any kind of 
health and safety violations.
  This is something that my colleagues Senator Blumenthal and Senator 
Booker and I and others worked on diligently, and we want to continue 
to make sure it is enshrined in this statute. And then medical coverage 
for 5 years post-eligibility and capping agents' fees at 5 percent.
  So my colleagues have said: Why do this? Why do this now? Well, that 
is because 94 percent of the DI--that is the biggest division of 
athletes; there are three divisions, and then other associations--94 
percent of those DI programs are in the red. So that is a pretty big 
issue.
  And the reason why it is so frustrating is there are four schools 
just in the State of Virginia that are putting exorbitant tuition fees 
on their students just to pay for college education. There are people 
that are cutting science programs, cutting professors, cutting other 
programs just to pay for college sports when, in reality, we need all 
those to beat China and compete--and not have an arms race in sports.
  So right now, the median debt for schools in the Big Four 
conferences--their median debt is $120 million. What are they doing? 
Now, I know everybody would like to blame it on coaches, and I 
guarantee you there are a few coaches that need to get called out. But 
the main reason for this is not because we are paying athletes. It is 
not because we have all agreed that they deserve to have part of this 
revenue. This number exists because there are no sound rules that 
prohibit an arms race from taking place and people who are billionaires

[[Page S5036]]

and millionaires putting any amount of money into their school program. 
And our bill fixes that and creates rules across the board for everyone 
to follow, so that we don't have this exorbitant arms race.
  Now, I will be the first to admit there is more to do on stadiums. 
There is more to do on other aspects of spending college sports. But we 
need to pass a bill tonight because we need to stop stealing money out 
of college and university institutions for education, for tuition, for 
professors, and putting it into an arms race on sports.
  It is time to make sports about playing sports again and not about 
the money. So this bill stops the cuts to women's and Olympic sports. 
Why? Because who is paying the bill right now?
  The No. 1 person paying the bill is a hundred programs and a thousand 
women and Olympic athletes because they don't make money, so the 
easiest way to save money in an institution is to cut them right now. 
And so we are trying to stop that. Our legislation says, yes, we are 
willing to give the college teams an antitrust exemption to pool their 
media rights and make more money, but only if they protect women's and 
Olympic sports. And just to be clear because there is a little 
misunderstanding about this: You don't get to have the antitrust 
exemption and then later cut these programs. You only get the antitrust 
exemption if you keep the women's and Olympic sports.
  If you don't do a pooling of media rights, we also have a provision 
for the next several years that makes people keep their women's and 
Olympic roster slots--so very important aspect of the bill.
  There is this big complaint about the amount of money going to 
coaches, and I welcome any discussion about how to fix this issue. And 
I want to thank my colleague Senator Lujan for the brilliant amendment 
that he offered in committee because, basically, it says you can only 
pay a coach--with fees from the education system--$500,000 a year and 
if you want to pay them any more than that, you have to go get somebody 
in the private sector to go pay that. So we are not going to be held 
hostage, as taxpayers, to these coaches' salaries. And in addition, we 
make it a requirement that you cannot transfer in the middle of the 
year.
  Now, a lot of people have said: Well, what are you going to do about 
the HBCU infrastructure because they are part of Division I football? 
You know, the SEC and others came along and said: Oh, just kick them 
out of Division I. We are like: No, that is a really bad idea. We are 
not going to push them out of Division I football. We are going to grow 
their capabilities in communication because, just like other people, 
they want their games seen, they want their broadcast, they want their 
broadband, but they don't have it. So this bill includes a $900 million 
authorization to help upgrade the schools that have been most impacted 
without the communications capabilities.
  A lot of my colleagues would say: Well, where is that student athlete 
support? I am very excited about a commission that we create in this 
bill to hear more from student athletes. But student athletes and their 
committees--and these are all the letters from student athletes from 
various conferences across the United States.
  Madam President, I ask unanimous consent that those letters be 
printed in the Record.
  There being no objection, the material was ordered to be printed in 
the Record, as follows:
                                                    June 15, 2026.
       Dear Chairman Cruz and Ranking Member Cantwell: As chair of 
     the American Conference Student-Athlete Advocacy Committee 
     (SAAC), I am writing to express our support for the Protect 
     College Sports Act of 2026. Recent legal, regulatory, and 
     economic changes have created significant uncertainty for 
     student-athletes, institutions, and conferences, and a 
     national framework is needed to ensure that college athletics 
     continues to serve current and future generations of 
     students. We support the Protect College Sports Act for the 
     following reasons:
       1. Student-athletes deserve clear and consistent national 
     rules. The current patchwork of state laws and court 
     decisions has created inconsistency across state lines and 
     within conference affiliation. Student-athletes should not 
     face different rights, obligations, or opportunities simply 
     because they compete in different states. A uniform federal 
     framework would provide greater transparency and consistency 
     for everyone involved.
       2. College athletics must prioritize educational 
     opportunities and the health and safety of student-athletes. 
     Student-athletes benefit from scholarships, academic support 
     services, medical care, nutrition programs, mental health 
     resources, tutoring, career-development programs, and other 
     benefits provided through intercollegiate athletics. Federal 
     legislation should preserve and strengthen these educational 
     benefits to ensure future generations continue to have access 
     to higher education opportunities and careers that may only 
     be accessible to them by participating in college athletics.
       3. Student-athletes should have opportunities to benefit 
     from their name, image, and likeness (NIL) while ensuring 
     fairness and transparency. NIL opportunities have provided 
     meaningful benefits for many student-athletes. However, the 
     absence of consistent national standards has created 
     uncertainty and inconsistent enforcement. Reasonable 
     disclosure requirements, transparent rules, and safeguards 
     against exploitation such as agent registration can help 
     ensure that NIL opportunities remain available while 
     protecting student-athletes from predatory practices.
       4. Congress should protect broad-based participation 
     opportunities. The strength of college athletics lies in the 
     thousands of opportunities available across men's and women's 
     sports. Student-athletes in Olympic sports, emerging sports, 
     and non-revenue sports deserve the same opportunity to 
     compete, earn degrees, and represent their institutions. 
     Federal legislation should help preserve these opportunities 
     and prevent unintended consequences that could reduce 
     participation or eliminate programs.
       We appreciate your bipartisan efforts to protect student-
     athletes, and we urge lawmakers to continue this important 
     work that expand opportunity, promote fairness and 
     transparency, and support long-term stability. Thank you for 
     your leadership and consideration of these important issues.
           Sincerely,
                                                     Joy Graziano,
                        Chair, Student-Athlete Advocacy Committee,
     American Conference.
                                  ____


   Statement of Support from the American Rivers Conference Student 
                   Athlete Advisory Committee (SAAC)

       Dear Senator Grassley, My name is Carson Strukel, and I am 
     a men's cross country and track and field student athlete at 
     Coe College, representing the student-athletes of the 
     American Rivers Conference at the Division III level.
       As a student-athlete who competes across two sports in the 
     American Rivers Conference (A-R-C), I want to express my 
     strong support for the Protect College Sports Act (PCSA) of 
     2026.
       I believe this legislation is essential to protect the 
     integrity of collegiate athletics across every sport. This 
     legislation addresses key areas that impact student-athletes' 
     day-to-day experiences, including NIL, eligibility, 
     transfers, injuries, representation, safety, and academics. 
     Each of these areas is incredibly important to address to 
     ensure the preservation of the integrity of collegiate 
     athletics across all sports. The PCSA allows for protections 
     over student-athlete privileges in each of these areas, which 
     I am confident is imperative for the optimization of the 
     student-athlete experrence in years to come.
       Although I am disappointed in the PCSA's neutral stance on 
     the question of student-athlete employment status, I 
     understand that this is a complicated issue that must be 
     further discussed. Despite the lack of protection over the 
     non-employment status of student-athletes, the PCSA still 
     provides an excellent framework for academic standards, 
     eligibility, and transfer regulations. The PCSA establishes a 
     proper baseline to apply these standards, leveling the 
     playing field with consistent and fair expectations for 
     college athletics across all NCAA divisions.
       As a current Division III student-athlete, knowing that 
     future student-athletes will be protected from unfair 
     competition while having room for more opportunities to 
     enhance their collegiate experience is beyond exciting. 
     Competition is not the only aspect of a student-athlete's 
     college experience and protecting that versatility is 
     essential for the development of young professionals in all 
     aspects of life. The PCSA lays a framework that will allow 
     student-athletes to succeed not only during their 
     eligibility, but also after their time in the NCAA concludes.
       I formally endorse the Protect College Sports Act as a 
     crucial step toward giving the NCAA additional stability to 
     continue creating opportunities for athletes like myself. I 
     welcome the chance to further engage with you and your staff 
     on issues affecting the student-athlete experience.
       Thank you for your time and dedication to improving college 
     athletics.
       As the representative of the American Rivers Conference 
     SAAC,
       Carson Strukel
       Coe College Athletics, Men's Cross Country, Men's Track and 
     Field.
       DIII National SAAC A-R-C Representative, Coe College SAAC 
     President.
                                  ____


Statement of Support From the Conference of New England Student-Athlete 
                           Advisory Committee

       Dear Senator Markey: My name is Davis Bone, and I am a 
     former ice hockey student-athlete at Johnson & Wales 
     University. I serve as the Conference of New England Student-
     Athlete Advisory Committee (SAAC)

[[Page S5037]]

     Representative, elected to represent the voices of thousands 
     of Division III student-athletes across our eleven member 
     institutions. Our conference spans institutions in 
     Connecticut, Rhode Island, Massachusetts, and Maine, giving 
     me the privilege of representing student-athletes from across 
     the region.
       I am grateful for the bipartisan efforts of Senators 
     Cantwell and Cruz in advancing the Protect College Sports Act 
     (PCSA) and for their continued engagement with student-
     athletes on issues impacting college athletics. Student-
     athletes need lawmakers to step up and ensure thoughtful, 
     effective policies that reflect their experiences and support 
     the long-term success of college sports. I appreciate the 
     steps this legislation takes to address current challenges 
     and strengthen the college athletics model.
       On behalf of the conference, we strongly support the bill's 
     protections for student-athletes of all divisions, including 
     transfer rights and NIL opportunities, with safeguards for 
     coaching changes, sports discontinuation, graduate transfers, 
     and cases involving sexual assault or harassment. We also 
     support NIL provisions such as agent registration, a national 
     registry, transparency measures, and fee limits, which help 
     protect student-athletes from exploitation. In addition, we 
     strongly support a standardized national NIL framework paired 
     with targeted antitrust protections to reduce legal 
     uncertainty and allow student-athletes, institutions, 
     conferences, and practitioners to develop and enforce 
     consistent, durable rules without constant litigation risk.
       As the Senate refines the legislation, we encourage 
     preserving governance pathways for recently graduated 
     student-athletes serving in high-level leadership, allowing 
     for sport-specific transfer windows, and strengthening 
     protections for women's and Olympic sports. We also believe 
     the bill can be strengthened to further provide the stability 
     it seeks by closing loopholes so states can't make laws to 
     skirt enforcement for violating rules and ensure the bill 
     applies to the especially disruptive current and pending 
     lawsuits, not just future ones. While the legislation remains 
     neutral on student-athlete employment status, this issue will 
     continue to shape the future of college athletics. Student-
     athletes across all divisions consistently express a desire 
     to preserve the educational model that makes college sports a 
     unique opportunity for academic, athletic, and leadership 
     development. As these conversations continue, we encourage 
     policymakers to prioritize broad-based participation 
     opportunities and the long-term sustainability of the 
     collegiate athletics model.
       I appreciate your continued engagement with student-
     athletes on legislation impacting college athletics. 
     Representing thousands of student-athletes, I remain 
     committed to partnering in these discussions and sharing 
     their perspectives. I look forward to continued collaboration 
     to ensure college athletics prioritizes opportunity, 
     education, competition, and student-athlete well-being. Thank 
     you for your consideration and for your dedication to the 
     future of college sports and public service.
           Sincerely,

                                                   Davis Bone,

                         Conference of New England Representative,
     DIII National Student-Athlete Advisory Committee.
                                  ____


Statement of Support From the Rocky Mountain Athletic Conference (RMAC) 
               Student-Athlete Advisory Committee (SAAC)

       Dear Esteemed Members of the United States Senate: My name 
     is Sean Belzer and I am a men's soccer player at the 
     University of Colorado in Colorado Springs representing the 
     Rocky Mountain Athletic Conference (RMAC) Student-Athlete 
     Advisory Committee. I write on behalf of thousands of 
     Division II student-athletes across Colorado, New Mexico, 
     Nebraska, South Dakota, and Utah to express our strong 
     support for the Protect College Sports Act (PCSA) of 2026.
       Division II athletics represents a unique and essential 
     part of higher education. Unlike many Division I 
     institutions, we do not compete for multimillion-dollar 
     television contracts and any other of high-profile revenue 
     opportunities. Rather, we focus on having a deep connection 
     within our local community. It means so much more to be a 
     part of an environment where we can represent our 
     institutions, serve our communities, and earn a valuable 
     education. Our student-athletes often balance rigorous 
     academics with athletics, campus leadership, employment, 
     internships, and community service. The Division II 
     philosophy emphasizes this concept of `life in the balance'.
       The Protect College Sports Act provides that stability and 
     balance. By establishing a consistent framework for name, 
     image, and likeness (NIL), transfer regulations, eligibility 
     standards, and student-athlete protections- it creates a 
     level playing field for institutions across every NCAA 
     division. The bill also preserves important academic 
     standards while providing protections for student-athletes in 
     areas such as health and safety, scholarship security, degree 
     completion, and governance representation.
       While much of the national conversation surrounding college 
     athletics focuses on Division I, it is important to recognize 
     that Division II is challenged in a different regard. Most 
     RMAC institutions operate with fewer financial resources, 
     smaller athletic budgets, and a stronger emphasis on 
     educational opportunity than commercial athletics. The 
     intentions behind legislation designed for Division I, often 
     overlook the unique experiences and needs of Division II 
     institutions. The PCSA acknowledges these differences by 
     protecting the educational model that has long defined 
     Division II while allowing student-athletes to benefit from 
     modem opportunities in a fair and sustainable manner.
       This legislation is particularly meaningful for the states 
     represented within the RMAC. A large majority of our student 
     athletes stay in the noted states upon graduation--becoming 
     teachers, healthcare professionals, coaches, business 
     leaders, military members, and public servants. Having a 
     strong Division II athletic program can contribute directly 
     to local economies, strengthen communities, and expand access 
     for thousands of students across the Rocky Mountain region. 
     Supporting Division II athletics means investing in the 
     workforce, leadership, and communities that help our states 
     thrive.
       We also appreciate that the Protect College Sports Act 
     remains focused on protecting student-athletes. In Division 
     II, the primary objective is to earn a degree while competing 
     at the highest level. The bill's provisions on NIL 
     transparency, agent accountability, academic protections, 
     healthcare benefits, governance reform, and competitive 
     fairness all support that goal. It ensures that student-
     athletes are supported not only during their playing careers 
     but long after graduation.
       As representatives of the Rocky Mountain Athletic 
     Conference Student-Athlete Advisory Committee, we urge you to 
     support the Protect College Sports Act. This legislation 
     represents a meaningful step toward creating a stable future 
     for college athletics while preserving the values that make 
     Division II unique. We appreciate your continued commitment 
     to listening to the voices of student-athletes and look 
     forward to working with you to ensure college athletics are 
     protected!
       If you have any questions or would like to hear more about 
     our experiences as Division II student-athletes, please reach 
     out. Thank you for your time, and consideration.
           Sincerely,
                                                      Sean Belzer,
     UCCS Men's Soccer, RMAC National SAAC Representative.
                                  ____

                                                   August 4, 2026.
       Dear Chairman Cruz, Ranking Member Cantwell, Senator 
     Schmidtt and Senator Coons: Established in 1987, the Thurgood 
     Marshall College Fund (TMCF) is the nation's largest 
     organization exclusively representing the Black college 
     community, TMCF rueluber schools include publicly supported 
     historically Black colleges and universities (HBCUs), 
     predominantly Black institutions (PBIs) and historically 
     Black community colleges (HBCCs). Our member institutions 
     represent 80% of all students attending HBCUs, Notably, 22 of 
     the 23 HBCUs playing Division I sports are represented by 
     TMCF.
       I am writing to join the commissioners of the Southwestern 
     Athletic Conference (SWAC) and the Mid-Eastern Athletic 
     Conference (MEAC) in support of the bipartisan Protect 
     College Sports Act. We are pleased that this bill will now 
     authorizes $900 million to enhance broadband capabilities at 
     HBCUs; however, the inclusion of this provision only 
     highlights the need for Congress to pass the bipartisan 
     IGNITE HBCU Excellence Act to address the comprehensive 
     deferred maintenance and infrastructure needs on our 
     campuses.
       HBCU athletics, along with the traditions and pageantry 
     that accompany them, are vital parts of the HBCU experience. 
     From the festivities of homecoming to the sounds of the 
     marching bands, many traditions associated with HBCUs are 
     directly linked to athletic competition. Increasingly, 
     however, a patchwork of state laws and the rapidly rising 
     costs of compliance have placed an undue burden on the 
     schools with the fewest resources. Creating a single federal 
     standard would allow these schools to remain focused and 
     devote every possible resource to their core mission of 
     educating the next generation of our country's leaders.
       TMCF stands ready to assist in this bipartisan effort to 
     provide stability to the ever-evolving landscape of college 
     athletics.
           Sincerely,
                                            Dr. Harry L. Williams,
     President & CEO.

                                         Thursday, August 6, 2026.
       Dear Chairman Cruz and Ranking Member Cantwell: On behalf 
     of UNCF (United Negro College Fund), I write to express our 
     enthusiastic support for the Protect College Sports Act and 
     to thank you for your exceptional bipartisan leadership in 
     advancing this landmark legislation. Throughout this process, 
     your willingness to engage stakeholders across higher 
     education and collegiate athletics has produced a thoughtful 
     framework that reflects both the complexity of today's Name, 
     Image, and Likeness (NIL) environment and the shared goal of 
     preserving opportunity for student-athletes and the 
     institutions that serve them. We are reassured by your 
     commitment to work with us this year concerning the lingering 
     issue of debt for many institutions wo represent.
       We are especially appreciative of the Cornmittee's 
     incorporation of provisions reflecting the objectives of the 
     IGNITE HBCU Excellence Act. By recognizing the unique role 
     Historically Black Colleges and Universities (HBCUs) play in 
     developing talent and expanding educational opportunity, this 
     legislation acknowledges an essential truth:

[[Page S5038]]

     HBCUs must not be left behind as college athletics evolve. 
     They must not be equipped to compete, innovate, and thrive 
     within a modem national framework with improved and up-to-
     date infrastructure.
       For more than 150 years, HBCUs have produced generations of 
     extraordinary scholar-athletes whose influence extends well 
     beyond athletic competition. They have become business 
     executives, military leaders, entrepreneurs, educators, 
     public servants, and community leaders who have strengthened 
     every sector of American society. As Congress modernizes the 
     NIL landscape, ensuring that HBCUs have equitable access to 
     the opportunities created by these reforms is not simply an 
     athletics issue. This is an investment in educational 
     excellence, workforce development, economic rnobiiity, and 
     America's longterm competitiveness.

                                                    July 15, 2026.
       Dear Chairman Cruz, Ranking Member Cantwell, and Members of 
     the United States Senate: We write as proud U.S. Olympians, 
     Paralympians, and Team USA Athletes who benefited directly 
     from the collegiate sports system, and who remain committed 
     to ensuring that future generations of athletes have the same 
     opportunity to compete at the collegiate level and represent 
     Team USA on the world stage.
       As the Senate considers the Protect College Sports Act, we 
     respectfully urge you to support this critical bill, 
     providing necessary opportunity safeguards for women and men 
     competing in collegiate Olympic and Paralympic sports.
       For decades, college athletics has served as the primary 
     pathway for Team USA athletes and the backbone of record-
     setting U.S. Olympic and Paralympic teams. Thanks to the 
     success of college football and basketball, Olympic and 
     Paralympic sports have been able to thrive. However, without 
     meaningful guardrails, there is a risk of unintentionally 
     eliminating these critical opportunities for broad-based 
     student athletes.
       The Protect College Sports Act recognizes the importance of 
     Olympic and Paralympic sports and includes several provisions 
     that provide meaningful stability for student athlete 
     opportunities in Division I sports and the collegiate 
     pipeline that supports Team USA:
       The Protect College Sports Act would help ensure that 
     athlete opportunities in collegiate Olympic and Paralympic 
     sports are not reduced as the college athletics model 
     continues to evolve.
       To preserve America's global leadership in sport and ensure 
     broad-based access for student-athletes, we respectfully 
     support the following elements of the legislation:
       Scholarship and roster protections for large athletic 
     departments. For institutions with annual athletics revenue 
     above $80 million, the bill maintains 2024-25 scholarship and 
     roster levels for Olympic and non-revenue sports, with 
     economic hardship waivers that requires cuts to revenue-
     generating sports' coaching compensation before cutting 
     scholarship and roster levels for women and men's collegiate 
     Olympic sports.
       Transitional protections for mid-sized athletic 
     departments. For institutions with annual athletics revenue 
     between $50 million and $80 million, the bill maintains 2024-
     25 scholarship and roster levels for four years, providing 
     important near-term stability for Olympic and non-revenue 
     sports.
       Division I sport sponsorship protections. The bill 
     preserves the current 16-sport minimum for FBS institutions 
     and 14-sport minimum for FCS institutions, while prohibiting 
     the NCAA or any successor governing body from lowering 
     Division I sport sponsorship or minimum participant 
     requirements.
       Paralympic walk-on opportunities and student-athlete 
     representation. The bill clarifies that collegiate athletic 
     programs may continue to accept walk-on Paralympic student-
     athletes without counting them toward roster caps.
       Athlete representation in intercollegiate governance. This 
     legislation mandates athletes have a seat at the table in 
     NCAA (or successor institutions) governance bodies which 
     helps elevate athlete voice in decision-making affecting 
     their futures.
       Together, these policies are not only critical to the 
     Olympic and Paralympic movement, and Team USA athletes--they 
     also reflect longstanding bipartisan priorities to promote 
     gender equity, and support student-athlete development across 
     all sports.
       At a time of significant change in college athletics, these 
     safeguards are necessary and will help protect scholarships, 
     roster spots, sport sponsorship, participation opportunities, 
     and athlete representation that are essential to sustaining 
     the next generation of collegiate student athletes--including 
     those aspiring to compete for Team USA.
       We encourage Congress to support the Protect College Sports 
     Act and to ensure athlete opportunities for current and 
     future generations of women and men's scholarship-supported 
     collegiate sports are not jeopardized.
       We stand ready to work with you and your colleagues as the 
     legislation advances to promote our shared commitment to 
     opportunity, excellence, and continued American leadership in 
     sport on the global stage.
       Thank you for your consideration and your leadership on 
     this important issue.

  Ms. CANTWELL. So the American Conference Student Athletes Advisory 
Committee, Rocky Mountain Athletic Association Student Athletes 
Advisory Committee, American Rivers Conference Student Athlete Advisory 
Committee, the New England Conference Student Athletic Advisory 
Committee--I could keep going and going and going.
  So there is support by athletes for this bill, but it is also 
endorsed by the United Negro College Fund because they believe that 
this bill will help stabilize one aspect of DI football that is really 
challenging, and that is the system.
  It is also endorsed by the U.S. Olympic Committee, and the reason it 
is endorsed by the U.S. Olympic Committee is because they know we are 
going to help preserve Olympic sports. Now, what are we going to do 
when we have the next Olympics or the one after that and you literally 
have not trained and skilled the athletes? Some of our biggest 
institutions are the ones who provide 70 percent of these athletes, and 
so if we don't stabilize and say ``stop cutting women's and Olympic 
sports,'' then we are going to be standing at an Olympics where we are 
not winning and going to ask why.
  I didn't want to deal with this issue, but when a kid from my State--
from a famous part of our State, White Pass, where the Mahre brothers 
won gold skiing in the Olympics because they grew up on that pass--
basically, this kid couldn't snowboard for us and went and snowboarded 
for Russia and won medals. I was like, this is a problem. So I am not 
for us losing in the Olympics because we undermine college athletics in 
an arms race because we don't want to put rules in place.
  So the Thurgood Marshall Fund also supports this. Team USA athletes 
all support this.
  And it is just time for us to get this issue over the top. That is 
really what we are talking about. It is not the final solution. It is 
putting a pin in the problems to stabilize the loss of women's and 
Olympic sports and to create the opportunity to discuss the larger 
structure moving forward. That is why I think we have so much support. 
It doesn't mean that we have solved every problem, but it means that we 
are on our way to solving the problems that exist and preserving the 
opportunities for the future.
  So let's get it over the top. I am very proud of this athlete, and 
she is a perfect example. Okay. She is No. 1 in the world right now, 
and she is the pole vaulter at University of Washington.
  But what if we continue to undermine the efforts of women's and 
Olympic sports? What if she doesn't have

[[Page S5039]]

that roster slot because we are too busy letting billionaires run the 
race and basically leave people behind? That is not what sports is 
about. It is about, any given day, the competition. So let's get back 
to playing sports again, and let's pass this legislation.

                Amendment No. 6828 to Amendment No. 6776

  Ms. BALDWIN. Mr. President, I call up my amendment No. 6828 and ask 
that it be reported by number.
  The PRESIDING OFFICER. The clerk will report.
  The senior assistant legislative clerk read as follows:
       The Senator from Wisconsin [Ms. Baldwin] proposes an 
     amendment numbered 6828.
  The amendment is as follows:

(Purpose: To modify the provision relating to the relationship of this 
                          Act to existing law)

       Strike section 121 and insert the following:

     SEC. 121. RELATIONSHIP TO EXISTING LAW.

       (a) In General.--No State or political subdivision of a 
     State may adopt, maintain, enforce, or continue in effect any 
     law, regulation, rule, requirement, or standard that--
       (1) conflicts with any provision of this title that would 
     prevent compliance with this title; or
       (2) governs, regulates, or invalidates policies or rules of 
     an institution, a conference, or an intercollegiate athletic 
     association that regulates--
       (A) the compensation to a student athlete or prospective 
     student athlete for the use of their name, image, or 
     likeness, except as necessary to comply with this title;
       (B) transfers of student athletes between institutions; or
       (C) any provision described in subsection (a)(1)-(3) or (b) 
     of section 113 relating to the eligibility of a student 
     athlete to participate in intercollegiate athletics.
       (b) Preservation of State Laws.--The following State laws, 
     rules, regulations, or requirements, or common law rights or 
     remedies shall not be preempted, displaced, or supplanted:
       (1) Except to the extent that such law, rule, regulation, 
     requirement, or common law right or remedy conflicts with 
     subsection (a), the following:
       (A) Uniform Athlete Agent Acts.
       (B) Civil rights laws.
       (C) Tort law, unless otherwise specified in paragraph (2).
       (D) Criminal law, unless otherwise specified in paragraph 
     (2).
       (E) Laws that relate to privacy or data breach.
       (F) Contract law.
       (G) Trademark law.
       (H) Copyright law.
       (I) Consumer protection law, unless otherwise specified in 
     paragraph (2).
       (2) Except to the extent that such law, rule, regulation, 
     or requirement, or common law right or remedy conflicts with 
     subsection (a)(2), any law, rule, regulation, or requirement, 
     or common law right or remedy, whether statutory or common 
     law, that gives rise to a cause of action for the following:
       (A) Personal injury, including psychological injury.
       (B) Wrongful death.
       (C) Property damage.
       (D) Sexual assault.
       (E) Harassment.
       (F) Fraud.
       (G) Injury resulting from abuse or hazing of a student 
     athlete, including physical or sexual abuse.
       (H) Injury resulting from deficient student or campus 
     safety measures.
       (I) Injury resulting from a failure to provide greater 
     protections to student athletes than those provided by the 
     health, wellness, and safety standards under subsections 
     (a)(1)-(4) or (b)(1)-(2) of section 107.
       (J) Any related cause of action that is derivative of any 
     of subparagraphs (A) through (I).
       (c) Rule of Construction.--To the extent liability for 
     violations of the antitrust laws is not limited by this 
     title, subsection (a) shall not be construed to preempt, 
     displace, or supplant the antitrust laws.
